The classifier the whole task was about accounts for **0.013% of the time its own path spends.** The 4.6 seconds are `preprocess` (4,573.7 ms), and specifically 3.2's text-layer separation: 7.2.1 argued its table was the cheap half of preprocessing because it skips region detection, arrowheads and skeletonisation, and **that argument is wrong by an order of magnitude** - producing `text_mask` needs the full binarization and layer decomposition, while the CLIP path only resizes to 224x224 and never binarizes at all (376.3 ms preprocess + 75.1 ms embed). **There is no fast path here to route to.** 13.3 proposed "NB fast prior -> ensemble classifier"; on these numbers the prior costs ten times what the ensemble it gates costs, and 7.2.5 independently measured it at -0.0091 macro F1 - two measurements, one of accuracy and one of latency, agreeing that Phase 13 should **route directly to CLIP + RBF SVM**. The general lesson in the form it came back: **a model's inference time is not its cost** - a 0.589 ms classifier sitting behind a 4.6 s feature pipeline, and the only number a user experiences is the sum. A genuine fast path would need a cheaper *feature*, not a cheaper classifier; the floor for anything that resizes and embeds is ~0.45 s, and 4.2's handcrafted table does not qualify either since it needs everything 3.2 produces and more | ✅ |

### 7.3 Hidden Markov Model — Sequential Diagram Parsing

**Core idea:** reading a diagram is a sequence. Hidden states = semantic roles; observations =
detected shape + text features at each visited node. Viterbi recovers the logical flow, which
becomes the code structure.

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 7.3.1 | State space definition | `{start, input, process, decision, branch-true, branch-false, loop-back, output, terminal}` | `src/parse/roles.py`: the nine states frozen in the plan's order, with `STATES.index(...)` the index every matrix in 7.3.4-7.3.12 is built on. The finding that shapes the rest of 7.3 is that **the plan's nine are not a subset of 2.1.5's 17-role node vocabulary - four of them do not exist as node annotations at all.** `branch-true`, `branch-false` and `loop-back` are properties of *how a node is reached* (the arrival edge), and `io` is one role covering both directions. That is not a flaw in the state space - it is what makes a sequence model worth building, since no per-node classifier can express any of them - but it means their supervision must be **derived**, and `derive_states()` is that derivation written down rather than buried. `coverage()` prices it over the 993 sequential labelled diagrams (693 hdbpmn + 300 fa_bresler, 14,056 nodes): **5,524 assignments - 39.3% - are rules rather than annotations**, four times what the state list suggests, because branches and loop-backs are 30% of all nodes. Two numbers carry forward: **only 105 of 2,897 branch assignments (3.6%) come from an edge label** - hdbpmn labels edges with message names and fa_bresler with alphabet symbols, so neither corpus ever writes "yes" on a page and the rest fall back to sibling ordering - and **every one of the 1,368 `loop-back` and 303 `output` rows is a derivation** with no human having said so. That is the honest ceiling on 7.3.9: for four states it scores against a human label and for five against this file's own definitions, so a model that reproduces `branch-true` perfectly has reproduced `derive_states`. The exercise stays real because `derive_states` uses what the HMM never sees - arrival edge, sibling order, DFS stack - so recovering it from `(shape, keyword, degree)` alone is still inference. Two judgement calls are recorded in `JUDGEMENT_CALLS`: **`event` -> process** (BPMN timers and message receipts, 12% of hdbpmn's labelled nodes, share `process`'s transition structure) and **`join` -> process** rather than `decision`, because a decision *chooses* an outgoing edge and a join does not - grouping them would corrupt the one transition row 7.3.10's repair rule depends on | ✅ |
| 7.3.2 | Observation model | Discrete: quantized (shape class, text-keyword class, in/out degree) | `src/parse/observations.py`: a factored discrete symbol, `shape-class \| keyword-class \| degree-class`, at 6 x 5 x 4 = **120 combinations** - too many for a 300-row state, so the working alphabet is the *observed* subset and 7.3.5 smooths only over that. **53 of 120 occur**, and the distribution inside them is steep enough to make the model estimable: **the top ten symbols carry 81.3% of all 14,056 observations** and only four symbols occur once, so the effective alphabet is nearer ten than fifty - which is what lets a 9-state discrete HMM be fitted from 993 diagrams at all. Restricting to observed symbols is not cosmetic: every unobserved one would be a column of pure smoothing constant in the emission matrix. The head is `box\|other\|linear` 19.9%, `round\|other\|linear` 10.5%, `box\|other\|source` 10.3%, `diamond\|empty\|branching` 9.1%. **The finding that matters for 7.3.11 is that the keyword factor - argued for as the one thing separating `start` from `terminal`, since both are a rounded box with degree 0 on one side - is mostly inert on this corpus**: `other` covers 69% of nodes and `question` only 0.8%, because hdbpmn's diamonds carry a gateway name rather than a question and fa_bresler's states carry a single letter. Its text also comes from the datasets' own transcription, so it encodes a **clean-text assumption** that 9.3's OCR on real handwriting is not obliged to reproduce - and 7.3.11 prices both. Position, size and colour are deliberately excluded: all three are strongly informative about role and all three are properties of *this scribe's layout habits* rather than the semantics, so an HMM using them would learn the corpus's dominant layout convention (8.6 clusters scribes precisely because those habits vary). The degree class carries the structural version of the same information and generalises | ✅ |
| 7.3.3 | Sequence construction | Convert each annotated diagram into an ordered observation sequence via topological/DFS ordering | `src/parse/sequences.py` -> `data/processed/sequences.json`: 993 labelled sequences (693 hdbpmn, 300 fa_bresler) holding **14,056 observations**, median length 14 and maximum 40, plus 3,000 unlabelled didi sequences kept for 7.3.6. **681 of the 993 contain a back edge (68.6%)** - all 300 state machines and 381 of the 693 BPMN pages - which is what justifies DFS over a topological sort: on a corpus where two pages in three contain a cycle, topological order is not a default with an occasional fallback, it is *undefined for most of the data*. The uncomfortable number is disconnection: **555 of 993 sequences (55.9%) are multi-component, giving 2,524 component breaks - 19.3% of all raw transitions**, far above the "occasional missed connector" the design anticipated, and almost entirely hdbpmn (554 of 693 pages against 1 of fa_bresler's 300). The cause is structural rather than damage: a BPMN diagram is drawn as several pools whose message flows are frequently not edges in the converted IR, so each pool arrives as its own component. Left in, those 2,524 pairs would have been **19% of 7.3.4's training data and pure noise** - the model learning the order unrelated lanes happen to be visited in - so they are recorded per sequence and excluded, which is the whole reason the field exists. The conversion's known cost is recorded rather than hidden: **a DFS linearisation puts one whole branch before the other, so a decision's two successors are never adjacent** - the model sees `decision -> branch-true` and then an unexplained arrival at `branch-false` many steps later, which is why 7.3.4's matrix is expected to be true-heavy despite the two being equally real in the graph. BFS has the mirror problem (siblings adjacent, the reader's path destroyed), and the path through a branch is what becomes code in Phase 12, so DFS is the reading taken of the plan's "topological/DFS" | ✅ |
| 7.3.4 | Transition matrix (supervised) | Estimated from labeled role sequences with Laplace smoothing | `src/parse/transitions.py` + `reports/figures/p7_hmm_transitions.png`: 10,539 transitions over 993 sequences, Laplace alpha = 1, component breaks excluded. The matrix is **sparse (42% of cells never observed) and low-entropy (2.26 bits against a uniform row's 3.17)**, which is the property that makes Viterbi worth running at all - the sequence carries real constraint. Three findings sit on top of it. **Two of the nine states have no outgoing transitions whatsoever**: `input` and `output` are never followed by anything, not rarely but never in 14,056 observations, because every node mapped to them is a BPMN data object and hdbpmn's converted IR gives data objects no flow edges - all 782 and 303 of them are isolated single-node components. Their rows are pure smoothing at 1/9, so Viterbi can reach those states only through the emissions and the initial distribution, and **whatever they score in 7.3.9 is a per-node classification result wearing an HMM's clothes.** Second, **the DFS bias 7.3.3 predicted is visible and large**: `decision -> branch-true` is 0.795 against `branch-false`'s 0.148, a 5.4:1 ratio that is a property of the linearisation rather than of how people draw decisions - the compensating fact being that `branch-false` carries the matrix's second-highest self-loop (0.253), since once the second branch is finally entered its whole subtree follows. Third, **excluding the component breaks mattered more than expected**: dropping 2,524 transitions moved one cell by **0.333** (mean 0.032), so they were *structured* noise rather than the uniform kind - hdbpmn pools end in a `terminal` and the next begins with a `start`, and `terminal -> start` would have become the matrix's confident lie. Finally the two diagram types do not share a structure - **a state machine uses 14 of the 81 possible transitions and a flowchart 47** (67 vs 34 empty cells; entropy 2.47 vs 2.19) - so the pooled matrix is an average dominated 8:1 by flowcharts, and `by_type()` returns them separately for 7.3.7 to test whether conditioning on Phase 5's 0.97-macro-F1 type prediction buys anything at decode time | ✅ |
| 7.3.5 | Emission matrix | Estimated from labeled shape ↔ role co-occurrence | `src/parse/emissions.py` + `reports/figures/p7_hmm_emissions.png`: a 9 x 53 matrix over 14,056 observations, alpha = 1, with **331 of 477 cells (69%) never observed** - the expected shape when the top ten symbols carry 81% of the mass. The deliverable beside the matrix is **the memoryless baseline it defines: reading each symbol's most likely state and ignoring the sequence entirely gets 65.15% (9,157 of 14,056 nodes)**, and it does so using only **seven of the nine states**, because two states are the argmax of no symbol at all and a memoryless rule can therefore never predict them whatever it sees. **That 34.85% is the entire budget available to the transition model** - anything 7.3.7 scores above 0.6515 came from the sequence, and anything below means Viterbi destroyed information the emissions already had. The ambiguity lands exactly where the plan hoped: `decision` is near-unambiguous (`diamond\|empty\|branching` at .73, 95% of its mass in two symbols) and `output` more so (`other\|other\|sink` at .84), but **`branch-true` and `branch-false` share the same most likely observation at almost the same probability** (`box\|other\|linear`, .46 and .43). A box with a plain label and one edge each way is what both look like because they *are* the same thing seen from a different parent - the distinction is which edge you arrived by, which is a property of the sequence and nothing else. **Those two states, 2,897 nodes between them, are what the HMM exists to separate and what no per-node classifier can.** Mean posterior entropy over symbols is 0.758 bits, which explains both why the memoryless baseline is as high as 0.65 and why the residual errors are concentrated rather than spread. One result is worth naming as luck rather than design: **`other\|*` - the catch-all bin 7.3.2 expected to be junk - is the most discriminative shape class in the matrix**, because hdbpmn's data objects convert to shapes outside the flowchart vocabulary and `input`/`output` are made entirely of them. The same alphabet on a different corpus would not have that | ✅ |
| 7.3.6 | Baum–Welch (unsupervised) | EM re-estimation on unlabeled sequences; compare to supervised | `src/parse/baumwelch.py` + `reports/figures/p7_baumwelch.png`: EM on 1,971 usable didi sequences (5,571 observations; 1,029 of the 3,000 pages hold fewer than two nodes and cannot be a sequence), 9 states, 53 symbols, tol 1e-4, 100-iteration cap, `init_params=""` so hmmlearn cannot silently overwrite the supervised start. **The experiment has a clean negative answer.** Started *at* the 7.3.4/7.3.5 matrices, EM improved the likelihood and walked away from the annotation: agreement ends at **0.3009 with only 4 of 9 states still matched to themselves** after Hungarian alignment, against 7.3.7's 0.7512 with the supervised matrices untouched - **EM on unlabelled data cost 0.45 of role-labelling accuracy while making the observations more likely.** Stated without hedging: **maximum likelihood and the annotation disagree about what the states should be**, and nothing in the EM objective knows it should care. The random start settles the other half - it reaches **-10,510.75 against the supervised start's -10,531.55**, so the supervised matrices are not even a good *optimisation* seed (a slightly worse basin than a random draw finds) and retain **0 states matched to themselves**; the semi-supervised reading is unavailable in both directions. The uniform run is included as a trap the task should be able to catch: it stops after **3 iterations** at a likelihood 1,337 nats worse, because a perfectly uniform `A`, `B` and `pi` is symmetric under permutation of the states and **Baum-Welch cannot break a symmetry it starts in** - every state stays identical, the posteriors stay flat, and the monitor reports convergence. **It is the one run of the three whose termination flag must not be read as convergence**, and its 0.3432 "agreement" is the alignment matching the largest true states, not a model that found anything. The conclusion for 7.3 is to use the supervised matrices: didi's 3,000 pages are the corpus's largest single source and contribute nothing, because a median didi page is 3 nodes - **5,571 observations against the labelled set's 14,056**, so the unlabelled set is not only unlabelled but *smaller*, and smaller in exactly the dimension that carries transition evidence | ✅ |
| 7.3.7 | **Viterbi decoding** | Most likely role sequence for a new diagram | `src/parse/viterbi.py` + `tests/test_parse_viterbi.py`: a hand-written log-space decoder, five-fold over the 993 sequences and 14,056 nodes. **The sequence is worth +0.103 accuracy and +0.148 macro F1 over the best memoryless decoder** (Viterbi 0.7512 / 0.7586 against 7.3.5's symbol-only 0.6483 / 0.6110, and a majority baseline of 0.3497 / 0.0576) - a real gain rather than a rounding one: **1,446 of the 4,943 nodes the symbol decoder got wrong are recovered by knowing what came before**, and the macro-F1 gain exceeding the accuracy gain says the recoveries are in the small states a memoryless rule cannot predict at all. Two knobs were swept and only one matters. **The Laplace constant does not**: accuracy runs 0.7504 / 0.7501 / 0.7509 / 0.7512 / 0.7509 across alpha 0.01 to 5.0, so 7.3.4's choice of 1.0 was safe and its stated reason - that smoothing keeps Viterbi's paths legal rather than fitting anything - is confirmed by the flatness; once no cell is zero the value is irrelevant. **Conditioning on the diagram type is worth 25 times the entire alpha sweep**: per-type matrices reach 0.7771 / 0.7763, +0.0259 accuracy over pooled, converting 7.3.4's finding that a state machine uses 14 of 81 transitions and a flowchart 47 - so Phase 13 should route Phase 5's 0.97-macro-F1 type prediction into the decoder rather than pool. The pooled 0.7586 falls short of the plan's 0.80 target, and the reason is structural and already visible: **every state in the space is missing one of the two sources of evidence** - `input` and `output` have uniform transition rows (7.3.4) so Viterbi decodes them on emissions alone, while `branch-true` and `branch-false` share their most likely symbol (7.3.5) so emissions alone cannot separate them. Correctness is pinned against `hmmlearn`: `decode()` matches `CategoricalHMM.decode` state for state on a two-state and a random nine-state model, and matches its path probability exactly on a deliberately symmetric model where both face a tie - **a brute-force enumeration over every path of length 7 confirmed that tie was real rather than a bug.** The log space is a correctness property rather than a preference: at these probabilities the plain recursion loses its last significant digit within a few hundred steps and underflows outright soon after, on graphs Phase 10 will assemble. **One defect found later by S4**: `decode_sequence` decodes each page as a single chain, including across the component breaks 7.3.4 deliberately excluded from the transition counts, so the decoder applies transitions the matrix was trained never to contain. It affects 555 of the 993 sequences and costs 0.0244 macro F1; `src/parse/s4.py` decodes each connected component separately and the correctness pinning against `hmmlearn` above is unaffected, since it is the segmentation of the input rather than the recursion that was wrong | ✅ |
| 7.3.8 | Forward / backward | Posterior marginals for per-node role confidence | `src/parse/posteriors.py`: log-space forward-backward checked against `hmmlearn`'s `predict_proba` and `score`, with `annotate()` writing `semantic_role`, `confidence` and `attrs.role_posterior` into the IR. The posterior argmax scores **0.7548 / 0.7625 against Viterbi's 0.7512 / 0.7586** - better by 0.0036, as it must be since it optimises exactly the quantity being scored - and **the two decoders disagree on 7.3% of nodes**. The gap is small enough that the choice falls to the other criterion: **Viterbi's path is guaranteed legal under `A` and the posterior path is not**, and Phase 10's validators are written against legal paths, so Phase 10 takes Viterbi's roles and this task's confidences. **The headline result is that the confidence is well calibrated** - mean confidence 0.7244 against 0.7512 accuracy, an overconfidence of **-0.027** and a Brier of 0.144, with **every band at or above its stated confidence** (0.378 -> 0.438, 0.599 -> 0.651, 0.800 -> 0.806, 0.958 -> 0.964, 0.994 -> 1.000). The model is very slightly *under*-confident and the miscalibration sits in the low bands where pessimism is harmless. Set beside 7.2.4's Naive Bayes claiming 0.975 confidence at 0.479 accuracy, that is **a 0.52 swing in honesty between two models in the same phase**, and the reason is structural rather than lucky: NB multiplies 14 correlated likelihoods and its sharpness *is* that double-counting, while a forward-backward marginal is a normalised sum over paths whose normalisation covers the same evidence, so nothing is counted twice. The practical deliverable is that **Phase 13's confidence gate has a usable operating point**: a gate at 0.9 accepts 33.0% of nodes and is right on **97.5%** of them, while the 21.8% below 0.5 are right on 44% and are exactly the population worth querying a user about. **The 1,486 nodes above 0.99 are correct on all 1,486** - a role the model is that sure of has not once been wrong in this corpus - which is a gate the 7.2.5 prior could not have supplied at any threshold | ✅ |
| 7.3.9 | Role-labeling accuracy | Per-role precision/recall vs. annotation | `src/parse/roleeval.py` -> `reports/role_labelling.md`: five-fold, per-type matrices, 14,056 nodes. **Macro F1 0.7763 against the plan's 0.80 - the target is missed by 0.024.** Five of the nine roles clear 0.80 (input 0.963, terminal 0.863, decision 0.859, output 0.831, process 0.807) and the failure is concentrated rather than diffuse: **dropping `branch-false` alone would put the macro at 0.804.** The task's real contribution is that **the verdict changes depending on which reference is used**: restricted to the four states a human actually annotated - `start`, `process`, `decision`, `terminal`, 8,706 of the 14,056 nodes - the model scores **0.8598, clearing the bar by 0.06**. That is not a way of claiming the target was met; it is a distinction the plan's own line cannot make, because the plan assumed the annotation would carry all nine states and 7.3.1 found it carries four. **Both numbers are reported and neither is the answer alone**: 0.7763 is what the pipeline achieves against the labels 7.3 defined, 0.8598 against the labels a human wrote. The confusions are exactly the ones the two matrices predicted: **`branch-false` loses 36% of its nodes to `process` (0.194) and `branch-true` (0.168)** - the pair 7.3.5 identified as sharing `box\|other\|linear` as their likeliest emission - so the evidence separating them is which edge the node was reached by, the alphabet does not contain it, and the transition matrix must carry the whole distinction while 7.3.4 showed the DFS ordering makes it enter `branch-false` five times less often. `loop-back -> process` at 0.257 is the same shape: a loop-back is an ordinary step that happens to be re-entered, and nothing about the node says so. **`start` at 0.692 is the one genuinely disappointing row** since it *is* annotated and has a distinctive emission; its 147 confusions with `loop-back` are fa_bresler state machines where the initial state is a circle that is also the target of a return transition, so the two states are the same node and which it "is" depends on a precedence rule 7.3.1 had to invent. What would raise it is **not more data and not more smoothing** - 7.3.7 showed alpha does nothing and 7.3.6 showed 3,000 extra unlabelled pages do less - but an **arrival-edge factor in the observation alphabet**, which would give `branch-false` its own signature and is exactly the information `derive_states` uses. That needs the assembled graph, so it is recorded as a concrete Phase 10 change rather than a hyperparameter to try. **Superseded in part by S4**: the 0.7763 is correct for the decoder as it stood, but that decoder ran Viterbi across the component breaks 7.3.4 excluded from training, and fixing that mismatch alone is worth +0.0244 - reaching 0.8003 without the arrival-edge factor this row asked for. The diagnosis above still holds for `branch-false`; what it missed is that 91% of the recoverable gain was in `input` and `output`, not in the branch pair | ✅ |
| 7.3.10 | Ambiguity repair | Use HMM posteriors to fix broken arrows (a decision node must have ≥2 outgoing) | `src/parse/repair.py`: three posterior-gated rules (a decision needs two ways out, a terminal does not continue, an orphan belongs to the flow), damage injected into all 993 labelled diagrams and scored against the edges deleted. **This does not work, and the failure is worth more than a working version would have been.** Best case across every configuration is **5.3% of broken arrows recovered against the plan's 80% target, at 5.8% precision - seventeen wrong edges invented for every one correctly restored**; at the 0.9 gate it is 1.0% recovery (16 of 1,609 at 10% damage) against 970 false repairs. Running this on a real page would degrade the graph badly. **The mechanism is a feedback loop between the two halves of 7.3**, and it is the finding that survives: at the 0.9 threshold the `missing_branch` rule - the plan's own example - **fires zero times in 993 diagrams**, because deleting a decision's outgoing edge changes its degree class from `branching` to `linear`, which changes its symbol from `diamond\|empty\|branching` (0.73 emission mass on `decision`) to `diamond\|empty\|linear` (0.22), so its confidence falls below the gate and **the rule that exists to repair damaged decisions is switched off by the damage**. Lowering the gate to 0.5 lets it fire 454 times and triples recovery to 5.3%; 7.3.8's calibration is behaving correctly, and the problem is that a well-calibrated confidence in a damaged graph is *correctly* low. Recovery stays poor even when the rules do fire because **a deleted edge leaves nothing behind** - the repair chooses among nodes by position alone, and on a 20-node BPMN page the nearest later node is right perhaps one time in fifteen. The `orphan` rule fires most (986 times at 10% damage) and is least accurate, since an isolated node in this corpus is usually a data object that was *always* isolated. The study is **a lower bound by construction and that is stated rather than used as an excuse**: deleting an edge is strictly harder than repairing a broken one, because a real broken arrow is a polyline with a gap that has two endpoints, a direction and a length - evidence 10.1.4's gap bridging can ask "does this stub point at that node" with, and which this damage model destroys. What Phase 10 must inherit is the loop, not the rules: **whatever repairs a decision must not be gated on that decision's own role confidence.** The rules are implemented, exercised and left in place with their measured numbers attached | ✅ |
| 7.3.11 | Ablation | Pipeline accuracy with HMM removed (bag-of-shapes baseline) | `src/parse/ablation.py`: a four-rung ladder over identical folds and 14,056 nodes. Against the plan's named baseline, **removing the HMM costs 0.454 macro F1** - a bag of shapes labels roles at 0.3228 and cannot do better, because six shape classes cannot address nine states. **But two thirds of that gain is the alphabet, not the sequence**, and reporting it as one number would credit the transition matrix with 7.3.2's feature engineering: adding the degree and keyword factors to the observation is worth **+0.2885** (a memoryless lookup table over the full triple reaches 0.6113 - 63% of the whole distance, with no transition matrix, no Viterbi and no training beyond counting), the Markov assumption is worth **+0.1473**, and per-type matrices a further **+0.0177**. **The single largest contribution in 7.3 is not the HMM.** The sequence model is worth a substantial +0.147 and it is the second-largest term. The factor ablation then confirms 7.3.2's prediction against itself: blanking one factor at a time from the 0.7763 model costs **0.2735 for shape and 0.2222 for degree** - both load-bearing and roughly equally so, which is the tidiest fact in the task, since what a node looks like and how it is wired matter about the same and neither alone passes 0.56 - while **dropping the text keyword makes the model very slightly better (0.7772, -0.0009).** 7.3.2 argued the keyword was the one factor separating `start` from `terminal`, then measured 69% of nodes carrying `other` and warned it was probably inert; it is **worse than inert**, adding emission columns that split counts without carrying evidence, so removing it concentrates the same mass into fewer, better-estimated cells. That is a result about *this corpus* rather than about the idea - hdbpmn labels gateways with message names and fa_bresler labels states with single letters, so neither writes the words the factor looks for, whereas the hand-drawn flowcharts saying "start" and "is x > 0?" that Phase 9's OCR will read are exactly where it would work. **It is kept with its measured contribution of zero recorded**, because removing it would bake this corpus's labelling conventions into the alphabet | ✅ |
| 7.3.12 | Continuous-emission variant | Gaussian-emission HMM on raw geometric features | `src/parse/continuous.py`: a GaussianHMM over nine raw geometric columns, 993 sequences and 14,056 nodes, same folds and same states as 7.3.7, transitions seeded from 7.3.4 and re-estimated - only the emission model changed. **The quantization cost nothing; it bought 0.50 macro F1.** Best continuous is `full` at **0.3118 accuracy / 0.2579 macro F1** against 7.3.7's discrete 0.7512 / 0.7586, a loss of **0.5007 macro F1** - the largest gap measured anywhere in 7.3 and more than three times the entire contribution of the sequence model (7.3.11's +0.147). The cause is the columns rather than the model: **`out_degree` has 53.9% of its mass on exactly 1 and 22.0% on exactly 0, with mean 1.078 and standard deviation 0.808**, so a Gaussian fitted to it spends real density on negative out-degrees and the `linear`-versus-`branching` gap that 7.3.5 showed identifies a decision is 1.0 - **only 1.2 standard deviations wide in the density the model is forced to use**; `text_length` is zero for 19.5% of nodes with a one-sided tail and fails the same way. 7.3.2 collapsed exactly these into four- and five-way classes, and the collapse was doing more work than it looked: **a bin boundary is a decision the discrete model gets to make and the Gaussian does not.** The continuous model has strictly *more* information - it can tell in-degree 7 from in-degree 3, which 7.3.2 explicitly discarded - and cannot use it. The covariance ordering matches the rest of the phase: `full` 0.2579 > `tied` 0.2455 > `diag` 0.1963, so modelling the correlation is worth **+0.062** even here, because `width`, `height` and `area` are three views of one measurement - the same mechanism 7.2.4 priced for Naive Bayes and 7.4.2 for the shape mixture, three models in one phase agreeing. **No fold failed at any covariance type**, including `full` at 54 parameters per state against 303 rows for the smallest, so the docstring's worry about singular covariances did not materialise. Phase 10 keeps the discrete emissions, and the number is recorded so the question is not reopened: **7.3.2's quantization is not a lossy compromise to improve on later, it is what makes the emission model work** | ✅ |

### 7.4 GMM + EM — Learned Shape Vocabulary

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 7.4.1 | Shape descriptor vector | Hu moments, vertex count, aspect, solidity, circularity, convexity defects, Fourier descriptors | `src/features/descriptors.py` -> `data/features/descriptors.parquet`: **12,400 shapes over 693 hdbpmn pages, 22 columns, no missing values and no constant columns** (6 of 12,406 labelled nodes yielded no closed outline). Labels are rectangle 5,947 / circle 3,231 / diamond 2,141 / freeform 1,081 - `ellipse` has zero rows, so 7.4.5 has four names to find, not five. **The headline finding is that hand-drawn shapes sit nowhere near their textbook descriptor values**: an ideal circle scores 1.0 on circularity and an ideal rectangle 0.785, while the drawn circles here average **0.520** and the drawn rectangles **0.215** - every class lands far below its ideal and in the same direction, because a photographed pen outline is rough and perimeter is squared in the denominator, so roughness is punished twice. `vertices` is the clearest casualty: a rectangle should be 4 and averages **8.0**, since Douglas-Peucker at 2% of a wobbly perimeter keeps the wobble. The *ordering* survives - circles really are roundest, rectangles least - so the signal is there and only the absolute scale is wrong, **which is the argument for 7.4.8 in advance: a template matcher keyed on ideal values would classify almost nothing.** By ANOVA F the strongest column is **`hu_1` at 2,693.9, twice the next** (circularity 1,411.6) and **32x `rect_aspect` (84.8) - the column built to measure elongation** - because a minimum-area rectangle is unstable on a rough outline while a moment integrates over the whole region; `rect_aspect` survives only as a rectangle detector, BPMN tasks averaging 3.26 against every other class near 1.2. **`hu_6` is inert at F = 1.1**, the skew-invariant moment that only changes under reflection, kept so the Hu block stays the standard seven rather than a subset chosen on this corpus. Two bugs were caught by the build and are recorded because the table was wrong until they were fixed: **the convexity-defect columns were dead for all 12,400 rows** (hull indices re-sorted before `convexityDefects`, which makes OpenCV raise, plus a guard that skipped the triangular hulls arrowheads have) - now F = 384.3, with diamonds averaging 4.04 defects against freeform's 2.20 - and **the textbook Hu log turned floating-point sign noise into the largest distance in the table**, measuring 60.0 between a square and the same square rotated 45 degrees against 22.5 between a square and a circle; the symmetric log now used puts that rotation pair at 0.0 while keeping square-versus-circle at 4.47 | ✅ |
| 7.4.2 | GMM fitting | Full / diagonal / tied covariance comparison | `src/parse/vocab.py`: a Gaussian mixture over 7.4.1's 22 standardised columns, K = 5, 12,400 rows, 5-fold, scored three ways that were allowed to disagree - held-out likelihood, BIC, and adjusted Rand against 4.1.3's names. **All three pick `full`, unanimously** (held-out -3.286 nats a node, BIC 55,601, ARI 0.1499 against `diag`'s -10.924 / 252,764 / 0.1365). The margin is not marginal: `full` beats the next best by **7.6 nats a node** and has a **4.5x lower BIC than `diag` despite six times the parameters** (1,379 fitted from 12,400 rows). **This is the exact opposite of 7.3.12's answer on the same corpus**, and the difference is the row-to-parameter ratio - there the smallest state had 303 rows for 45 parameters, here there are nine rows per parameter and the columns are correlated enough that modelling the correlation pays for itself. 7.4.1 explains why: `hu_0`-`hu_3` are moments of one region, and `rect_fill`, `extent` and `solidity` are three normalisations of one area, so a diagonal covariance is required to pretend that is all independent - **7.2.4's finding in a different model.** **`tied` is the informative failure: worse than `diag` on held-out likelihood (-19.828 vs -10.924) while carrying more parameters**, because one shared scatter matrix must average two different elongation axes - circles vary in roundness, rectangles in aspect - into something fitting neither; it also produces the most lopsided partition, one component of 81 nodes against another of 7,021. **The number that matters more than the winner is that the best adjusted Rand is 0.1499 and the best purity 0.4584, against a majority-class baseline of 0.4796** - so **no covariance type produces components that match the declared shape names better than always guessing `rectangle`.** That is not a failure of the comparison but the finding it exposes: the mixture describes the cloud well and the structure it finds is not the shape distinction a person draws, because 7.4.1 measured rectangles at aspect 3.26 against ~1.2 elsewhere, so **a wide task and a square task are further apart in this space than a square task and a diamond.** The mixture is finding size and elongation where the labels encode identity, and 7.4.5 must do its naming from a weak alignment rather than a strong one | ✅ |
| 7.4.3 | Component selection | BIC/AIC sweep over K ∈ [3,15] | `src/parse/selection.py` + `reports/figures/p7_gmm_selection.png`: K from 3 to 15, `full` covariance, 12,400 rows, 5-fold. **The four criteria pick four different K and the disagreement is the result** - BIC elbow **6**, BIC minimum 13, AIC minimum 14, held-out likelihood 15 (still rising at the end of the sweep), best label agreement 9. **What settles it is a column none of the criteria look at: from K = 9 onward the smallest component holds one node** (66 at K = 7, 9 at K = 8). Every component past that is a spike on a single outlier, and a one-node Gaussian with a regularised covariance has an enormous density at its own point while contributing nothing elsewhere - **which is exactly how BIC keeps improving while the vocabulary gets worse.** So BIC's minimum at 13 and the likelihood's preference for 15 are not evidence for a 13- or 15-shape vocabulary but evidence that a mixture with enough components can memorise outliers; `reg_covar` bounds the damage without removing the incentive. **The plan's instruction to take the BIC *elbow* rather than its minimum is vindicated by this**: K = 6 is the last K before the components start collapsing, and it is the answer carried forward. The two criteria move in opposite directions across the whole sweep - **purity falls monotonically from 0.4731 at K = 3 to 0.2887 at K = 15 while held-out likelihood rises from -6.217 to +2.167** - which is the sharpest available statement of 7.4.2's single-K finding: **more components do not buy more shapes, they buy finer cuts of the same shapes.** And the ceiling is low everywhere: the best purity at any K is 0.4731 against a majority-class baseline of **0.4796**, so **no K in the plan's range names shapes better than always saying `rectangle`**, and the best adjusted Rand (0.1589 at K = 9) is 0.009 above what K = 5 already gave | ✅ |
| 7.4.4 | EM convergence study | Log-likelihood curves, init sensitivity (k-means++ vs. random) | `src/parse/emfit.py` + `reports/figures/p7_gmm_em.png`: the log likelihood recorded after every EM iteration, four initialisations x five seeds, K = 5, `full` covariance, 12,400 rows. **All twenty runs were monotone** - not one iteration in any trajectory decreased the likelihood - so EM's guarantee survives `reg_covar` and float64 here, which is worth having checked rather than assumed. **The plan's expected ordering does not appear.** Plain `random` reaches the best likelihood of any initialisation (-0.1828) and `kmeans` the worst best-case (-0.3211), with the whole range across all four at **0.14 nats - smaller than the spread within any single one of them**, so the initialisation *strategy* is not what decides the answer. **The seed is: the best-to-worst gap across all runs is 3.3512 nats a node**, against the 7.6 nats that separated the best covariance type from the second best in 7.4.2. **Nearly half the effect of the biggest modelling decision in 7.4 can be produced by changing a random seed**, and any single fit announcing "the GMM found five shapes" is reporting one draw. The per-init detail is the useful part: **`kmeans` has the largest spread (3.2129) while converging fastest (23.2 iterations against k-means++'s 38.4)** - the most expensive initialisation is the least stable, buying speed and paying in variance - and **`random_from_data` has the smallest spread (1.1665) by a wide margin**, because seeding means on actual rows cannot place a component where the data never goes, which is the failure mode the other three all have available. This is recorded as a caveat on the rest of 7.4: every fit in 7.4.2, 7.4.3 and 7.4.5 uses `n_init=1` at a fixed seed, so their comparisons remain like-for-like but **their absolute likelihoods carry a few nats of uncertainty, and 7.4.3's held-out curve rising to K = 15 should be read with that in mind.** The practical recommendation is `random_from_data` with several restarts - half the spread of anything else, and its best within 0.04 nats of the overall best | ✅ |
| 7.4.5 | Cluster → shape naming | Map discovered components to rectangle / diamond / oval / arrow / blob | `src/parse/shapenames.py` -> `reports/shape_vocabulary.md` + `reports/figures/p7_gmm_montage.png`: K = 6 (7.4.3's elbow), `full` covariance, 12,400 shapes, each component given two independent names - the label most of its nodes carry, and a geometric name from its mean descriptor. **Not one of the six components has its two names agree.** Four of the six are majority-`rectangle`, only three of the four labels are ever dominant, and `freeform` is dominant nowhere. **The finding behind that is visible in one column: the mixture split the corpus by outline quality, not by shape.** Components 2, 3 and 5 have mean circularity **0.064** against components 0, 1 and 4 at **0.459** - a **7x gap**, where the widest gap between two actual shape classes is 2.4x (circle 0.520, rectangle 0.215) - and mean solidity 0.30, far below the lowest class mean of 0.574. A circularity of 0.06 is not a shape: it means the perimeter is enormous for the area, which is a ragged, broken or fragmented outline. **32.6% of the corpus - 4,047 nodes - is in that condition**, and its label mix (3,071 rectangle, 365 diamond, 314 circle, 297 freeform) is roughly the corpus distribution, so those three components are not three shapes but **one damage mode cut three ways**. The mixture's dominant axis of variation is therefore **how well 3.2 extracted the outline rather than what was drawn inside it**, which is 7.4.2's ARI of 0.1499 and 7.4.3's monotonically falling purity seen directly instead of inferred. **The naming rules are not what failed**: components 2, 3 and 5 genuinely are blobs at solidity 0.30 and the rules say so correctly - what disagrees is the annotation, because **a fragmented rectangle is still labelled `rectangle`**, the human having labelled the intent while the descriptor measured the pixels that survived binarization. Component 5 is the sharpest case, simultaneously **the purest component in the table (95% rectangle) and the least shape-like** (aspect 6.97, solidity 0.410): long thin BPMN pools whose outlines broke up. Two of the plan's five names are unreachable and are recorded as such - **`oval` has zero rows in hdbpmn and `arrow` is an edge with no bounding box** - and a third, `freeform`, is unreachable by any threshold because its measured means sit *between* the other classes on every separating column. The consequence is carried forward explicitly: the responsibilities 7.4.6 feeds to the HMM carry real information that is **mostly not shape identity**, and any template matcher 7.4.8 pits against this vocabulary is competing with something that has largely learned image quality | ✅ |
| 7.4.6 | **Soft assignment usage** | Feed GMM responsibilities as soft shape evidence into HMM emissions instead of hard labels | `src/parse/softshapes.py`: 7.4.2's learned components substituted for the IR's annotated shape factor in 7.3.2's symbol, 693 hdbpmn flowcharts and 12,581 nodes, K = 6, five folds, three modes. **Integrated and ablated, and the answer is that it should not be wired in**: `annotated` scores **0.7506** macro F1, `hard` (argmax component) **0.5192** and `soft` (responsibility-weighted) **0.5124** - **the learned vocabulary costs 0.2314 macro F1 and softening it costs a further 0.0068.** The soft form being *worse* is the informative part, because it was the half with a real argument behind it: a node whose outline fragmented is one the mixture is unsure of, and spreading its evidence should help. It does not, and 7.4.5 explains why - **uncertainty in these responsibilities is not uncertainty about which shape but about which of three near-identical damage components a broken outline belongs to** (mean circularities 0.056, 0.060, 0.072), so a spread node is hedging between three descriptions of the same ragged blob rather than between a rectangle and a diamond, and averaging those emission rows only flattens the likelihood. **Both halves were made soft** - responsibility-weighted counts at training as well as a mixture emission at decode - so this is not the familiar artefact where soft decoding from a hard-counted matrix looks good because it is really just smoothing; the tests assert the two matrices differ and that a one-hot responsibility makes the soft decoder reproduce the hard one exactly. The alphabet grew from **53 symbols to 120** (six components x twenty surviving `keyword\|degree` tails) and carried less: the annotated factor's six classes were chosen to mean something, while the six learned components mostly encode how well 3.2 extracted the outline, so the emission matrix spends twice the columns on a distinction that does not separate roles. 181 nodes have no descriptor because their outline never closed, and are given a uniform vector rather than dropped, since dropping one would renumber the sequence and desync its states. **What this settles is that the shape factor's value comes from being a semantic category, not from being a partition of the descriptor space** - a statement about this table rather than about the idea, since a descriptor set robust to broken outlines might yet earn its place | ✅ |
| 7.4.7 | Per-scribe adaptation | Fit GMM per drawing style; show everyone draws rectangles differently | `src/parse/scribes.py` + `reports/figures/p7_scribe_covariance.png`: 5,947 rectangles from **105 scribes** (91 with 30+ rectangles, median 59 each), holding the shape class fixed so the question is about the hand rather than about which exercises a writer was given. **The plan's claim is true and much smaller than it sounds.** It is true: a random forest names which of 91 writers drew a rectangle **9.42% of the time against 1.10% chance and a 1.82% majority baseline - 8.6x chance**, from geometry alone with the writer's own page held out, so a rectangle really does carry evidence about whose hand drew it. It is small: the strongest scribe-separating column is `hu_0` at ANOVA **F = 13.7**, where 7.4.1's strongest shape-separating column on the same descriptors is `hu_1` at **2,693.9** - **shape identity is roughly 200x the effect scribe identity is.** The ranking underneath is informative: `extent`, `defect_count` and `rect_fill` lead the scribe list (12.9, 12.3, 12.0) while `rect_aspect` is near the bottom at 1.8, so **what varies between writers is how cleanly the box closes, not how wide they draw it** - the aspect is dictated by the BPMN task text that must fit inside, which is the exercise and not the hand. **Adaptation, the thing the plan actually asks for, makes it clearly worse: -7.9299 nats a rectangle** (global -31.5818 against per-scribe -39.5117) over 285 scribe-fold blocks and 4,723 held-out nodes, because a diagonal Gaussian over 22 columns is 44 free parameters and the median scribe brings 59 rectangles, so the personal model fits its own noise while the pooled one does not. The `unseen scribe` control reports **0 blocks, correctly** - held out by writer, nobody has training rows of their own, so no personal model can exist, which is precisely the deployment case and why the adaptation figure must never be quoted without it. One methodological correction is recorded: the first measurement fitted each model inside its own scaler and compared the likelihoods, which is invalid because a per-scribe scaler and a global scaler are different changes of variable whose densities differ by an unrelated Jacobian; it reported -16.84, and with **one scaler per fold shared by both models** the honest figure is -7.93 - the positive control asserting that per-scribe fitting *does* win when scribes are far apart and data plentiful is what caught it. For 8.6 this is evidence *for* its design: the signal is real, and clustering writers into a few style groups buys parameters per cluster rather than per person | ✅ |
| 7.4.8 | vs. template matching | Compare learned vocabulary against hardcoded shape templates | `src/parse/templates.py`: 4.1.3's hand-written `classify()` against 7.4.2's learned mixture against a supervised ceiling, on the same 12,400 shapes over 693 pages, five folds grouped by page. **The learned vocabulary beats the hand-written one by 0.0426 macro F1 (0.4160 against 0.3734) - and both leave about half the available signal on the floor**, because a random forest on the *same 22 descriptors* reaches **0.8160**. So the honest headline is not "learned beats template" but that **the descriptors support a 0.82 vocabulary and neither candidate delivers one** - shape naming on this corpus is not hard, it is unsolved by these two methods. Reporting the +0.0426 alone would have been reporting the gap between two failures, which is exactly what the ceiling row exists to prevent. The two win in different places: **the template is the better diamond detector (0.6245 against 0.3275)**, the one clear victory for a hand-written rule, because 4.1.3's `DIAMOND_EXTENT` is a threshold on precisely the axis-aligned `extent` that 7.4.1 identified as the only rotation-sensitive column, applied to the one shape whose signature *is* rotation - a rule written by someone who had looked at the pictures - while the mixture has no such prior and scatters diamonds across components that mostly encode outline quality. The learned side wins on `rectangle` (+0.21) and `circle` (+0.22), the two largest classes and the two whose clouds are big enough for a component to land on. **Both collapse on `freeform` (0.0932 and 0.1319) where the supervised model gets 0.5666** - 7.4.5 predicted the failure, since freeform's means sit between every other class on every separating column, but not the recovery: **freeform is separable in this table, just not by a rule or by a cluster centre**, because what identifies it is a conjunction rather than a region. One deployment failure is priced separately: **predicting `ellipse`, a class hdbpmn does not contain, costs the template 0.08 macro F1** (strict 0.2934 against collapsed 0.3734), a fifth of its score, and none of it is a bad geometry rule - it is a vocabulary written against a different corpus. The learned predictor cannot make that mistake by construction, since its names come from a Hungarian assignment against training labels. **Neither vocabulary should ship as a shape classifier**: where Phase 10 needs a shape name from pixels, the supervised model on 7.4.1's descriptors is the thing to use, and 7.4.6 already showed Phase 10 does not need any of this where an annotation exists | ✅ |

---

# Phase 8 — Unsupervised Clustering & Style Adaptation (Unit 4)

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 8.1 | K-means on components | Cluster detected components by visual similarity | `src/cluster/kmeans.py`: K-means over 7.4.1's 22 descriptors, 12,400 shapes, 693 pages, K in {5, 6} x {k-means++, random}, ten restarts a cell. K-means is the hard, spherical, equal-weight limit of 7.4.2's mixture, so the task is not "do these cluster" - 7.4 settled that - but **what the dropped covariance and soft assignment were worth: 0.0717 ARI against the mixture's 0.1499, on the same rows and scaler, so the spherical assumption costs more than half.** **Neither beats a constant**: best purity 0.4485 against a majority baseline of 0.4796, making this the third independent method to land under it after 7.4.2 and 7.4.3's whole sweep. **The partition reproduces 7.4.5's damage axis from a completely different algorithm** - cluster 0 is 2,827 nodes (22.8% of the corpus) at circularity **0.033** and solidity **0.219**, and it is **80% `rectangle`**, the purest cluster in the table and pure in *breakage* rather than geometry. A hard spherical partition finding the same dominant axis as a full-covariance mixture promotes that from a property of the GMM to **a property of the table**. **Only three of the five clusters are real**: cluster 4 holds 2 nodes and cluster 3 holds 123, and at K = 6 the smallest is a single node - the same collapse 7.4.3 documented. That also inverts the usual initialisation story: **k-means++ scores better (+0.0161 ARI) while producing the *more* degenerate clusters** (2 members against random's 100), because seeding on squared distance is precisely a procedure for putting a centre on an outlier. The seed control comes out the other way from 7.4.4's: with ten restarts the ARI spread across five seeds is **0.0137** and seeds agree with each other at **0.8788** mean ARI, so **K-means is reproducible on this table where the mixture was not** - the one respect in which the simpler model behaves better, and no rescue, because a stable answer that loses to a constant still loses to a constant | ✅ |
| 8.2 | Elbow + silhouette | Choose K rigorously | `src/cluster/choosek.py` -> `reports/figures/p8_choose_k.png`: K from 2 to 15 on the 12,400-shape descriptor table, inertia elbow and silhouette side by side with ARI, purity and the smallest cluster size. **The two unsupervised criteria agree - both choose K = 3 - and at K = 3 the ARI against the shape labels is 0.0028.** At K = 2, the silhouette's best-separated partition in the whole sweep (0.4168), the ARI is **-0.0001**: to four decimal places the cleanest structure these 22 columns contain is *exactly independent of what shape was drawn*. The criteria are not unreliable; they measure separation faithfully, and the well-separated structure here is not shape. **The two curves move in opposite directions at precisely the informative step** - between K = 3 and K = 4 the silhouette suffers its largest fall in the sweep (0.4300 -> 0.2374) while the ARI makes its largest rise (0.0028 -> 0.0823), so a reader holding only the silhouette panel stops one K short of the first partition that knows anything. **The ARI peak at K = 15 (0.1359) is where the sweep stopped, not where the answer is**: two clusters there hold under ten nodes and the smallest holds one, so the gain is bought by carving outliers into trivially pure singletons - and **purity, not being chance-corrected, tells the truth by falling** (0.4525 -> 0.3681) where ARI can be bought. That is 7.4.3's collapsing-component artefact in a different currency and with the opposite sign, caught by the same guard: print the smallest cluster size beside the criterion. **Chosen K = 4** - the purity maximum, the ARI maximum below the dissolution point, the largest K with no cluster under ten members, and incidentally the number of shape classes hdbpmn contains, which nothing in the sweep was told. Two caveats stated with it: its purity of 0.4525 is **still under the 0.4796 majority baseline**, and it disagrees with 7.4.3's elbow of 6 for the mixture on the same table - not a contradiction but the expected consequence of a different objective with a different degeneracy, and the reason **"the" K for a corpus is not a well-formed quantity** | ✅ |
| 8.3 | **Hierarchical clustering** | Ward-linkage dendrogram: shapes → {box-like, circular, pointed} → {rectangle, rounded-rect, square, …} | `src/cluster/hierarchy.py` -> `reports/figures/p8_dendrogram.png`: Ward linkage over 12,400 shapes and 22 standardised descriptors, cut at 2/3/4/6/8 with a nesting test at every transition. **The plan's hypothesis - shapes -> {box-like, circular, pointed} -> {rectangle, rounded-rect, square} - is refuted at the root merge.** The three-way cut is not three geometric families: it is 3,444 nodes at circularity **0.045** and solidity **0.284** (78% `rectangle`), 8,836 intact ones, and a 120-node oddity. **The top of the tree is damaged/intact, not box/round/pointed** - 8.1's cluster 0 and 7.4.5's components 2, 3 and 5 found for a third time by a third algorithm - and no cut level recovers a taxonomy from a root split about image quality. **Deeper cuts refine the same axis rather than naming shapes**, which is what the refinement test exists to show: parents whose children carry *different* dominant labels number one of two at 2->3, one of three at 3->4, one of four at 4->6, and **none of six at 6->8**. By the eight-way cut the tree is subdividing a continuum of outline integrity at finer thresholds - a ranking, not a taxonomy. The one thing the hierarchy tells us that a flat partition could not is **where shape first appears: the fourth level down**, at 4 -> 6, when the intact cluster finally splits into a diamond-dominant 2,472 (44% diamond) and a rounder 6,364, taking ARI to its peak of **0.0843** - six times the K = 4 figure and still an eighth of a supervised model on the same columns. Two disqualifications are recorded for the deeper cuts: **a singleton appears at K = 4 and never leaves**, and by K = 8 a whole cluster is spent on **18 nodes at rect_aspect 163.9 with two vertices**, degenerate slivers from failed contour extraction. And the tree's own fidelity is poor - **cophenetic correlation 0.4737**, under half the variance of the distances it was built from - so the cut levels are where a variance criterion chose to cut rather than natural joints, which is the difference 8.4 then prices against the three rules that do optimise distance | ✅ |
| 8.4 | Linkage comparison | single / complete / average / ward | `src/cluster/linkage.py`: single / complete / average / ward over the same 12,400 shapes, same scaling, same cut at K = 4, so the rule is the only variable. **The two rankings are exactly inverted** - by cophenetic correlation average (0.9291) > complete (0.8202) > single (0.7771) > ward (0.4737); by agreement with the shape labels ward (0.0148) > average (0.0003) > single (0.0000) > complete (-0.0011). **The rule that describes this geometry best partitions it worst, and the reverse.** **Three of the four rules did not produce a partition at all**: single, complete and average each put **over 99.8% of the corpus in one cluster** and spend the rest on 1-18 nodes - single's 12,397/1/1/1 is textbook chaining, and on a corpus with a heavy tail of fragmented outlines the chain has no gap to stop at, while complete and average, whose biases are supposed to be opposite, land in the same place. **Ward is recommended purely because it is the only rule that partitions** (3,444 / 8,836 / 119 / 1), and it loses every unsupervised column. **Both internal criteria reward the degeneracy by large margins**: single linkage's silhouette is **0.9563** against Ward's 0.4052, because three points far from one enormous blob is precisely what a silhouette maximises - a reader choosing a rule by silhouette picks the one that clustered nothing, by 0.55. **Purity does it more quietly**: average scores 0.4799 and single 0.4797, both above Ward's 0.4615 and both at the **0.4796 majority baseline**, because a Hungarian assignment mapping one 12,389-node cluster to `rectangle` *is* the majority classifier, reproduced to four decimal places. Taken with 8.2's silhouette peak at a K whose ARI was -0.0001, this establishes the phase's standing caveat rather than a one-off: **on this table internal validity criteria systematically prefer the partitions carrying the least information**, because compactness and separation are maximised by isolating exactly the fragmented outlines that 8.1, 8.3 and 7.4.5 all identified - which 8.8 then inherits rather than rediscovers | ✅ |
| 8.5 | DBSCAN | Discover outlier and novel shapes outside the vocabulary | `src/cluster/dbscan.py` -> `reports/figures/p8_outlier_gallery.png`: DBSCAN over the 12,400-shape descriptor table, `min_samples` 44, eps from the k-distance elbow (4.5301) with a five-point sweep around it. **DBSCAN never produced a vocabulary** - at every setting but the smallest it returns one cluster holding 98-99% of the corpus plus a thin rind of noise, which is 8.4's single-linkage result with a noise label attached, as predicted, since DBSCAN *is* single linkage with a density threshold: the threshold does not cut the fragmented-outline bridge, it shaves its ends. Halving `min_samples` moves the noise share by 0.05 points, so eps is the only lever, and **the noise fraction swings 16-fold (0.35% to 5.55%) across a four-fold change in eps** - which is why no single number here describes the corpus. **The outliers then contradicted the design's own prediction.** The prediction was badly extracted rectangles; the enrichment is `diamond` **1.59x** and `circle` **1.49x** over corpus share, with `rectangle` **depleted at 0.59x** and `freeform` - the one class that would plausibly mean "novel shape" - depleted at 0.65x. And **one column's mean would have described 118 of the 142 backwards**: outlier `rect_aspect` averages 23.28 against a clustered 1.95, reading as "elongated slivers", while the *medians* are 1.07 against 1.36, so the typical outlier is more square than the typical clustered shape. Reported as two subsets rather than one average: **24 slivers** (16.9%, all labelled `rectangle`, two vertices, circularity 0.012 - the extraction failures 8.3 isolated at aspect 163.9) and **118 compact outliers** (83.1%) which at the same circularity as the corpus carry **more vertices (9.79 vs 8.77) and more convexity defects (4.21 vs 3.36)** - extra internal structure inside an ordinary outline. **The gallery names it: BPMN gateways with their X and + markers, and events with message, timer and terminate glyphs.** The novel shapes the plan asked for exist and are not new geometry - **they are BPMN's own iconography, which hdbpmn's four labels flatten**, an exclusive gateway being annotated `diamond` exactly like a plain one. That is why `diamond` and `circle` are enriched and `freeform` is not: these are shapes the annotation *under*-describes, not shapes the geometry cannot handle. Concrete for Phase 10: **where the label says `diamond`, the marker inside it is recoverable and carries the gateway's semantics** - the difference between an exclusive and a parallel branch, and so between two different generated programs. The honest limit is the count: 118 of 12,400 is what a 1.15% noise rind surfaces, a demonstration that the signal is separable rather than a detector, and building one is a supervised problem whose labels this corpus does not contain | ✅ |
| 8.6 | **Handwriting style clustering** | Cluster scribes by stroke statistics (slant, thickness, curvature, spacing) for per-cluster OCR adaptation | `src/cluster/styles.py` -> `data/features/scribe_styles.parquet` + `reports/figures/p8_style_clusters.png`: eight stroke statistics computed over 3.2.8's **text layer** (the writing, separated from the drawn shapes, so this is not 7.4.7 again) for 692 hdbpmn pages and **105 scribes**, median 8 pages each, each writer represented by the median of their pages. **K = 3 chosen** - the best-separated partition with at least three clusters and none under five writers, since K = 6 scores marginally higher and strands one scribe alone. Silhouettes are 0.18-0.21 throughout and that is the honest reading: handwriting is a continuum and these are three regions of it. Cluster 0 (41 scribes) writes **large and straight** (height 0.0258, curvature 0.301), cluster 2 (52) **small, dense and round** (curvature 0.373, density 0.285, tightest spacing), cluster 1 (12) **small, wide and far apart** (aspect 1.56 against 1.09/1.13). **The plan named the two statistics that turned out not to matter**: by ANOVA across the clusters, `curvature` 56.04, `height` 50.20, `aspect` 35.21, `spacing` 20.17, `density` 18.83 - and **`slant` 3.01 (p = 0.054) and `thickness` 2.30 (p = 0.106) are the only two that fail to separate**. Thickness is a pen and a camera, and these writers used similar pens. Slant fails for a specific, reportable reason: **53.9% of pages measure exactly 0**, because the classical shear-variance estimator needs long vertical strokes and BPMN labels are two or three short words, so the projection profile is nearly flat and the search returns its centre - the estimator is right and the corpus's writing is too short to support it. What separates writers here is **how round and how large they write**. **Both controls are reported: the exercise control passes outright** at AMI **-0.0080**, chance-corrected for "no relationship at all", so eleven modelling prompts left no trace in these columns despite directly changing how much text a page carries - the control most likely to fail. **The page control passes with a bound**: requiring *all* of a writer's pages to land together gives 0.2772 against a 0.0159 chance rate (17.4x), while the quantity 8.7 actually needs - can a page be routed to its own writer's head - is the **modal share, 0.7927 against a 0.3333 chance rate**. So four pages in five route correctly and **one in five does not**, which bounds what 8.7 can achieve. The OCR gain the plan pairs with this row is measured in 8.7 rather than here, because a gain quoted by the task that chose the clusters would be quoted by an interested party. For 7.4.7 this closes its loop: per-scribe models cost 7.93 nats on 59 rectangles and 44 parameters, and **these clusters hold 41, 12 and 52 writers**, so a per-cluster model has 12-52x the data | ✅ |
| 8.7 | Style-conditioned OCR | Route text crops to the OCR head fine-tuned for that style cluster | `src/ocr/styled.py` -> `reports/figures/p8_style_ocr.png`: 9,807 real hdbpmn text crops with BPMN-annotation transcriptions, **held out by writer** (26 test writers, 79 training, 7,599 / 2,208 crops), `trocr-small-handwritten`, four arms - zero-shot, one global head, three heads on 8.6's style clusters, and **three heads on random writer groups of the same three sizes**. **The plan's >= 15% CER improvement is not met; the measured effect is 31% in the wrong direction.** zero-shot **1.0573** CER, global **0.6628**, random **0.8210**, style **0.8704**. **The random control is what makes this an answer instead of an excuse**: without it, style losing to global has two explanations and no way to choose. **Dividing the training set into three costs 23.9% relative CER on its own** - the price of the architecture, not of 8.6's clusters - and **8.6's clusters then cost a further 6.0% on top**, so grouping writers by how they write is measurably *worse* than grouping them at random, the opposite of the design's hypothesis. **The mechanism is visible per group and every style head loses to the random head of comparable size** (0.8078/0.9288/0.8834 against 0.7809/0.8411/0.8572): a style cluster is by construction a *narrow* slice of handwriting - cluster 2 is 52 writers who all write small, dense and round - so its head sees less variation than a head trained on 52 arbitrary writers, and at test time it faces a writer it has never seen, which is the combination that punishes specialisation. The style split is also the more lopsided one (794 crops in cluster 1 against 1,064 in the smallest random group), and that head is the worst in the table. **Exact match separates the arms more sharply than CER**: 0.1245 global against 0.0154 and 0.0086, one crop in eight exactly right against one in sixty-five and one in a hundred and sixteen. The one unambiguously positive number is that **fine-tuning removes 37.3% of the zero-shot error** - the zero-shot CER exceeds 1.0 because TrOCR, trained on single lines of IAM cursive, inserts more than it gets right on two-word block capitals wrapped inside a drawn box. **This is the third independent measurement of the same shape**: 7.4.7's per-scribe Gaussians cost 7.93 nats against a pooled one, 7.4.3's components collapsed onto outliers as K rose, and a per-style OCR head costs 24-31% relative CER - every time this corpus has been asked to support a specialised model the pooled model has won, and always for the same reason. **It is not evidence that style conditioning cannot work in principle**: the result most directly recommends per-cluster *adaptation from the global head* rather than independent fine-tuning, since that removes the 23.9% arithmetic penalty and leaves only the question 8.6 asked. **8.6's clusters are not thereby worthless** - they passed their own controls - but a real description of handwriting style is not, at this data scale, a useful thing to condition an OCR head on | ✅ |
| 8.8 | Cluster validity | Silhouette, Davies–Bouldin, Calinski–Harabasz | `src/cluster/validity.py`: silhouette, Davies-Bouldin and Calinski-Harabasz over 18 partitions - K-means at K = 2..15 and 8.4's four linkage rules at K = 4 - with **ARI carried in the same row as the answer key**, since none of the three indices can see a shape label and a metrics table without one would be decoration. **Not one of the three is even neutral.** Silhouette correlates with ARI at **Spearman -0.8411 (p â‰ˆ 0)** and Davies-Bouldin at **-0.7049 (p = 0.0011)**: both are strongly and significantly *anti*-correlated with the truth, so a reader would do better minimising them. They are built on entirely different constructions - a per-point margin against a worst-case spread-to-separation ratio - which rules out an idiosyncratic flaw in either. **Both name `single k=4` as the best partition in the table**, which is 12,397 nodes plus three singletons; Davies-Bouldin scores that **0.0547**, where under 0.5 normally reads as excellent. **Calinski-Harabasz is not neutral either - its pooled -0.0774 (p = 0.76) is two opposite errors cancelling**, and the within-family split proves it: inside K-means all three are negative (sil -0.7055, DB -0.4242, **CH -0.7187**, since CH falls mechanically with K from 4,882 to 2,198 while ARI rises), while inside the linkage family **CH is the only index that scores +0.80 and correctly picks `ward`**, rejecting the three collapsed rules at 426-611 against K-means' 2,200-4,900 because its dispersion ratio explicitly penalises empty clusters. The pooled coefficient describes neither half and is exactly what a one-line metrics table would have reported. **None of this says the indices are broken**: they measure compactness and separation correctly, and 8.1, 8.3, 8.5 and 7.4.5 established that the compact, well-separated structure in these 22 columns is outline integrity, so faithfully finding the best-separated partition finds the one isolating the most damaged outlines. The rule that survives, and the one to carry out of 8.x: **an internal validity index should be used here only to reject partitions, never to select one** - CH's rejection of the collapsed rules is correct and useful, and every selection all three made was wrong | ✅ |
| 8.9 | Cluster stability | Bootstrap resampling agreement (ARI) | `src/cluster/stability.py`: 25 bootstrap draws per setting, refit from scratch, compared with the reference partition **on the unique shared rows** - so a repeated bootstrap row cannot agree with itself for free - across K-means and Ward at K in {3, 4, 6}. **These partitions are stable, and that is the useful half**: K-means at K = 3 recurs at **0.9875** mean ARI across independent resamples and even the worst row recurs at 0.5540, so **nothing in 8.1-8.5 is an artefact of which 12,400 shapes happened to be photographed** - the damage axis, the collapsed linkage rules and the outlier rind are properties of the corpus. The structure is real; it is simply not shape. **Stability is then a fourth criterion whose point estimates point the wrong way**: Spearman against agreement with the labels is **-0.5508**, the most reproducible partition (kmeans K = 3, 0.9875) agrees with the labels at **0.0028**, and the most informative (ward K = 6) is the **least stable at 0.5540**. **That correlation is over six rows at p = 0.2574 and is explicitly not claimed as a finding** - it is consistent with 8.2, 8.4 and 8.8, and 8.8 established the same relationship at -0.84 with p < 0.001 over eighteen partitions, which is the sample size that can support it. **Ward is the less stable algorithm at every K, by 0.19-0.24, despite having no seed at all** - the tests assert its determinism - so all of its instability is sensitivity to *which rows are in the sample*, which is the more serious kind: restarts bought K-means' seed spread down to 0.0137 in 8.1, and nothing buys sample sensitivity down, because agglomerative merges commit early and irreversibly on whichever pair is closest. **K-means at K = 4 shows what fragility looks like from the inside**: mean 0.8842 with sd **0.1479** and a worst draw of **0.4884**, the widest spread of any K-means row - and K = 4 is 8.2's recommendation and the K-means row that agrees most with the labels, so the informative partition is again the fragile one. **Stability therefore joins silhouette and Davies-Bouldin on this table: useful for rejecting a partition that will not reproduce, useless for selecting the one that means something** | ✅ |
| 8.10 | Unlabeled-set discovery | Cluster unlabeled diagrams to surface possible new diagram types | `src/cluster/discovery.py` -> `reports/figures/p8_discovery.png`: 1,695 real photographs, CLIP embeddings, labels withheld, in three experiments designed around 6.1.1's warning that **source is predictable from the embedding at 0.9776-0.9918** and source and label are nearly the same variable. **(1) All real pages: type beats source at every K, by 0.09-0.16 AMI** (0.7968 vs 0.7068 at K = 2), so the worst case - a clustering that recovers datasets and nothing else - is ruled out; stated with its own caveat, since the three sources map almost one-to-one onto types and type is therefore a refinement entitled to score higher on granularity alone. **(2) The chaos corpus alone is the decisive test and it passes**: 260 pages, five types, one capture protocol, so anything recovered is content - **unsupervised K-means reaches AMI 0.8147 and ARI 0.7250 with no labels of any kind**, against 6.1.1's *supervised* 0.9925 macro F1 on the same embedding and pages. **This is the strongest evidence anywhere in the project that Phase 6's classifier is not merely recognising which dataset an image came from**: 6.1.1 argued it from a supervised control and left open that the labels were doing the work; here there are none, and the types are visible in the geometry of the representation itself. The gap to 0.9925 is what the labels are worth and it is real - at K = 4 two of the five types have already merged. **(3) hdbpmn alone - 704 pages, one type, one source, the only place a new sub-type could appear - surfaced none.** Agreement with the `exercise` a writer was set runs -0.001 to 0.085, which is nothing, so the sub-clusters are neither eleven prompts in disguise nor new types. **What they track is the scribe, at AMI 0.2242 - about three times the exercise column at every K and flat across K** - so a CLIP embedding of a photographed flowchart carries identifiable information about whose hand drew it, and within one type that is the dominant remaining axis. That corroborates 7.4.7 from an entirely different representation (there a rectangle's 22 descriptors named its writer at 8.6x chance while shape was 200x the effect; here, with type held constant, the writer is what is left standing) and feeds 8.6 directly, since page-level style structure has to exist before a style-clustering task can start. **Discovery works for the question it was asked and not below it**: a taxonomy of diagram types is recoverable without labels at roughly three quarters of supervised agreement once provenance is controlled, and a pipeline mining this corpus for a sixth type would find handwriting styles and mistake them for one | ✅ |

---

# Phase 9 — CNN Component Detection & Handwriting OCR (Unit 4)

**Goal:** the perception backbone — find every component, read every label.

### 9.1 Detection Backbone

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 9.1.1 | Detector choice | YOLOv8 / RT-DETR fine-tune vs. Faster R-CNN; justify | `src/detect/choice.py` + `src/detect/metrics.py` -> `docs/detector_choice.md`: all three families the plan names, fine-tuned from COCO-pretrained checkpoints on 9.1.2's export under one fixed budget - same 1,842 pages, same boxes, 12 epochs, 896 px, one card, no per-arm search - because a justification that is only an argument is worth less than one that is a measurement. **The methodological point is that every arm is scored by one evaluator rather than by its own framework's reporting**: ultralytics prints a mAP computed after its own confidence filter and its own matching order, torchvision prints nothing, and taking each at its word compares reporting conventions rather than detectors, so `metrics.py` implements COCO AP once and each arm's only job is to emit `{image, cls, xyxy, score}`. **The architecture barely matters: the spread across all three arms is 0.0142 mAP@0.5** - yolov8n 0.8913, fasterrcnn 0.9034, rtdetr-l 0.9055 - and **every arm puts all seven annotated shape classes above 0.98**, with `double-circle` at exactly 1.000 for all three. That is 6.3.7's result in a different task (six classifiers inside 0.012; three detector families inside 0.014) and the same conclusion: the model was never the binding constraint. **The entire spread lives in one class** - RT-DETR beats yolov8n on `arrowhead` by **+0.1412** (0.4863 against 0.3451), which accounts for essentially all of its lead, and on the hand-drawn slice that is 0.8971 against 0.8580 - so what **10.6x the parameters and 5.8x the training time** buy is nothing on shapes and a seventh of a point on the 15-pixel class. Two per-class figures are labelled noise rather than quoted: **`parallelogram` has 7 validation instances** and reads 0.835 / 0.883 / 1.000, a 0.165 spread one box can produce. **The control that had to be run came back clean and is reported anyway**: the first pass left torchvision's `box_score_thresh=0.05` and `box_detections_per_img=100` alone and Faster R-CNN returned **9,847 boxes against RT-DETR's 92,400** - not a tidier detector, a censored one, since AP integrates the whole ranking and a framework discarding its own tail is scored on a truncated recall curve, the identical mistake the ultralytics `conf=0.25` default would make. Rerunning at 0.001/300 to match the other arms nearly doubled the boxes to 17,017 and moved mAP@0.5 by **+0.0001**; the truncation was harmless here because the discarded tail was all false positives below the useful part of the ranking, but that could only be known by measuring it. **The choice is recorded with the condition for reversing it**: RT-DETR-l is the most accurate detector and **yolov8n is what 9.1.3-9.1.7 are built on**, because it is within 0.0142 pooled, trains in a sixth of the time on the single card 9.1.5 and 9.1.7 both need for further runs, and **RT-DETR's whole advantage is a class 9.1.5 attacks directly** - so if tiling leaves the arrowhead under RT-DETR's 0.4863 the 5.8x is worth paying and this decision should be reversed, which is a measurement 9.1.5 makes rather than an opinion. Latency does not enter it: 32.3 / 35.0 / 57.1 ms a page against 6.1.2's 26 ms of CLIP extraction, so the trade is about Phase 15's retraining cadence, not about what a user waits for | ✅ |
| 9.1.2 | Class set | Shape classes + arrowhead + text-block + UI widget classes | `src/detect/classes.py` + `src/detect/dataset.py` -> `data/processed/detect`: **eight classes frozen** - rectangle, rounded-rect, diamond, circle, double-circle, parallelogram, freeform, arrowhead - over **2,312 pages and 54,256 boxes**, with the admission rule written down rather than assumed: a class needs a box in the frame of an image that exists on disk, enough instances to reach all three splits, and a definition a human could check. **Two of the four groups the plan names do not survive that rule, and finding out now is the task's main product.** **The UI widget classes cannot be built at all**: sketch2code is the corpus's only wireframe source with a role vocabulary and it is the largest annotated source in the project - **37,372 nodes over 484 pages, more annotated nodes than every other source combined** - and its IR carries `"geometry": "absent"`, because the structure came from real HTML that was never rendered, so **not one of those 37,372 nodes has a box**. Phase 16's wireframe route needs a rendering pass or a box campaign, and until one exists 9.1.4's mAP says nothing about wireframes. **`text-block` exists and is the wrong text**: 175 boxed instances on 115 pages, all of them hdbpmn `textAnnotation` margin notes, and **every one has an empty `text` string** - the label text 9.3 has to read is annotated as a string *on a node* in every source and never as its own region, so training this class would teach the detector to find staples rather than labels.
