Three more shapes are excluded with counts: `ellipse` has zero boxed rows anywhere (7.4.1 found the same), `octagon` is DIDI-only and DIDI's coordinates are a graphviz layout *fitted* to the ink extent rather than an observation, and `line`/`arrow` are polylines rather than regions. **`arrowhead` survives only as a derivation and says so**: there is no arrowhead annotation in any source, so 13,240 heads are placed at hdbpmn's sequence-flow waypoints - the last waypoint is the arrival point on the target's boundary, which is where the head was drawn - with a square extending back along the final segment, sized at 0.30x the page's median node height because **nothing in the annotation records how large anyone drew a head**, so arrowhead AP measures that convention as much as the detector. The derivation is restricted to hdbpmn on purpose: **fa_bresler's polylines are raw ink traces whose direction is whichever way the pen happened to move**, so it contributes states and no heads, which is why `arrowhead` appears on 693 of the 2,312 pages. The source mix is a decision with a stated cost - hdbpmn 693 real photographs, fa_bresler 300 real pen trajectories rendered onto synthetic paper, flowchartseg 1,319 **computer-rendered** pages kept because they are the only source of `parallelogram` and they triple `rectangle` - so **only 30% of the corpus is a photograph of paper**, splits are inherited from 1.3.3 rather than re-derived (re-deriving hdbpmn's would break the writer-disjointness every score in Phases 5-8 rests on) and fa_bresler, which never entered the manifest, is split by its 25 scribes under the same rule. One number sets up 9.1.5 before it starts: pages export at a 1,280px long side, and **the median derived arrowhead is 15.6 px there** against a median node of some hundreds | ✅ |
| 9.1.3 | Training run | Anchors / imgsz / epochs; mixed precision on RTX 4500 | `src/detect/train.py` -> `experiments/detect/final/weights/best.pt`: the plan names four knobs and **one of them does not exist for this architecture**, which is the first thing recorded - **YOLOv8 is anchor-free**, its head predicting a DFL distribution over distances from each cell to the four box sides, so the only size prior is the 8/16/32 px stride of the three levels. The honest substitute is to ask what an anchor set *would* have been and whether the strides reach it: k-means over 41,893 training boxes returns anchors from **[16.4, 15.2] to [790.8, 417.5]** at 896 px, a 50x range of scales in one corpus, and **the smallest cluster is the arrowhead cluster**. Only 0.84% of boxes fall under one P3 cell so the finest level can represent nearly everything, but **35.2% fall under one P4 cell, and per class that is 91.25% for `arrowhead`** against 0.11% for `rounded-rect` and 0.00% for `diamond` and `double-circle` - median short side **11.2 px** for a head against 48.2 and 105.9 for those. **The arrowhead is not merely a small class, it is a class that lives on a single feature level while every other class is described by all three**, and that is the mechanism behind every arrowhead number in 9.1. **imgsz is the biggest knob and it is almost entirely the small class**: at 10 epochs, 640 -> 0.7972, 896 -> 0.8683, 1280 -> 0.8729, so the first step is worth **+0.0711** and the second only +0.0046 and the pooled metric saturates by 896 - while `arrowhead` runs **0.2157 -> 0.3085 -> 0.3826**, a monotone **+0.167** still climbing where the pooled curve has flattened. Resolution buys pixels for the class that has none and averaging hides it, which is 7.1.4's argument for macro F1 in a different currency. **Mixed precision is not free, and this contradicts the usual claim**: at 896 with everything else fixed AMP trains **1.166x faster and scores 0.005 lower** (0.8683 against 0.8733) - a 17% speedup for half a point, worth taking for a sweep and worth reconsidering for a final run, and worth *measuring*, since 0.1.2 recorded bf16 as a capability and this is the first time it has been priced under load. **The long run reaches mAP@0.5 0.9107** (hand-drawn slice 0.8787, `arrowhead` **0.4318**) at 40 epochs and 1280, and the epoch axis at that size is 10 -> 40 for **+0.0481**, so the last thirty epochs are worth ten times the last resolution step. **That settles part of 9.1.1's open question in an unexpected direction**: 9.1.1 named RT-DETR's arrowhead AP of 0.4863 as the threshold for reversing its choice, and the same yolov8n at 40 epochs and 1280 reaches **0.5197** - past it without tiling, without 10.6x the parameters, and at a fifth of RT-DETR's training time even on this longer schedule. That is **not** a claim that yolov8n beats RT-DETR, which was never run at this budget and would presumably also improve; it establishes that **a large part of 9.1.1's architecture gap was a budget artefact rather than an architectural one**, which is worth knowing before a 12-epoch bake-off is read as a statement about model families. One measurement is disqualified rather than quoted: **per-page latency across the sweep cells is contaminated by cold start** - each cell pays CUDA and cuDNN warm-up on its first page and the 1280 final reads 19.4 ms against the 1280 sweep cell's 35.4 ms at the same input size - so 9.1.5 adds a throwaway forward pass before timing and these figures are an upper bound that must not be compared with each other | ✅ |
| 9.1.4 | Detection metrics | mAP@0.5, mAP@0.5:0.95, per-class AP | `src/detect/report.py` -> `reports/detection.md` + `reports/figures/p9_detection.png`: 9.1.3's weights over 308 validation pages, 37,837 predictions. **The plan's target is met on all four readings** - pooled **0.9107**, hand-drawn only **0.8787**, annotated classes only **0.9791**, loose IoU 0.25 **0.9511**, against a bar of 0.80. Four views rather than one because 9.1.2 established this corpus can answer the question four ways, and picking one and calling it "the" mAP is the move 7.1.2's OOB-metric trap and 8.8's validity indices were both about. **The four disagree by 0.088 and every disagreement is informative.** Dropping `arrowhead` moves the headline from 0.9107 to 0.9791, so **the derived class costs 0.057 of the pooled mAP on its own** and a reader given only that number would not know seven of eight classes are effectively solved. Restricting to the hand-drawn photographs costs 0.030 at IoU 0.5 and **0.158 at 0.5:0.95** (0.7813 -> 0.6230), because a computer-rendered rectangle has an exact boundary and a photographed pen stroke does not - so **any single mAP quoted for this detector is three-quarters a statement about pages nobody drew**. The loose threshold rescues `arrowhead` from **0.5197 to 0.7934**, a gap of **0.274** and the largest of any class by a factor of nine, which is 9.1.2's size convention being priced: four heads in five are *found* and only half of those land inside IoU 0.5 of a box whose size was chosen by a rule rather than measured. **`arrowhead` at mAP@0.5:0.95 is 0.1534 and that is near the arithmetic floor rather than a model failure** - 9.1.3 measured the median head at 11.2 px, an IoU of 0.75 there requires the prediction correct to about one pixel a side, and IoU 0.95 is unreachable at that scale by anything that quantises coordinates, so averaging ten thresholds to 0.95 for this class measures resolution rather than detection; that is the number Phase 10 should cite when asking how far to trust a head's *position* as against its existence. **The strict threshold reorders the classes into a legibility ranking**: at IoU 0.5 seven classes sit between 0.927 and 1.000, while at 0.5:0.95 they spread from `double-circle` 0.9974 and `rectangle` 0.9572 down to `circle` 0.7846 and `freeform` 0.6959 - not how well each class is recognised but **how well its boundary is defined**, a ruled rectangle having unambiguous edges where a hand-drawn circle and a BPMN data object do not, which is 7.4.1's drawn-circles-at-circularity-0.520 finding seen from the other side. `parallelogram`'s 0.9274 rests on **7 instances** and is not a measurement of anything | ✅ |
| 9.1.5 | Small-object handling | Arrowheads are tiny — tiling / higher input resolution | `src/detect/tiling.py`: five arms over 128 hdbpmn validation pages on 9.1.3's weights - three inference resolutions, tiling with the page-trained weights, and tiling with a model retrained on an 11,358-tile export. **The Definition of Done is not met and is reported as a failure: nothing beats the baseline's arrowhead AP@0.5 of 0.5197**, the best alternative being 0.5134 from simply inferring at a higher size. The two remedies fail for two different and separately useful reasons. **Higher resolution than the model trained at makes things worse, and worst for the biggest class**: 1600 loses 0.006 on the head and collapses `rectangle` from **0.9081 to 0.6976**, because at 1600 a BPMN pool is larger than any box the 1280-trained model has seen and a detector generalises downward in scale far better than upward - **so the resolution knob is bounded by training rather than by the image**, and 9.1.3 could raise the head by training bigger where this task cannot by inferring bigger. **Tiling's failure is structural and is the finding worth keeping: tiling destroys every class larger than a tile.** `rectangle` scores **0.0277 naive and 0.0327 trained** against the baseline's 0.9081 - a near-total loss retraining does not touch - while every other class recovers to within 0.03 of baseline once the model is trained on tiles (rounded-rect 0.964, diamond 0.978, circle 0.972, freeform 0.957). The mechanism is the export's own 60%-inside rule: **an hdbpmn pool is wider than 640 px so it satisfies that for no window at all** and vanishes from the labels, which is what 32,411 box-tile pairs dropped at seams actually means. Tiling is a fix for corpora where everything is small, not for pages whose objects span two orders of magnitude. **The naive arm earned its place**: page-trained weights on tiles score 0.5411 against the trained tiler's 0.7232, so **the scale mismatch alone is worth 0.18 mAP**, and a version of this task without the retrained arm would have concluded tiling does not work when what it measured was that a 4.5x magnification the model never saw does not work. **One thing tiling wins, which is why the loose threshold is reported**: at IoU 0.25 `tile_trained` reaches **0.8199, the best arrowhead figure of any arm**, above the baseline's 0.7934 while losing at 0.5 - it **finds more heads and localises them worse** against 9.1.2's convention box, which is the more useful direction for a Phase 10 that needs to know a head exists near a polyline end rather than where its box is to a pixel, but it costs **16.5x the latency** (564 ms against 34 ms, 23.7 forward passes instead of one) and is not a trade to take for one class. **The honest summary is that 9.1.3 already did what this task was asked to do**: arrowhead AP ran 0.2157 -> 0.3826 across the resolution sweep and reached 0.5197 with the longer schedule, so input size and training length moved it 0.30 while neither remedy here moves it at all - the class is bounded by 9.1.3's anchor finding (91% of heads under one P4 cell, median short side 11.2 px) and by 9.1.2's derived box, and **the fix that would work is better supervision rather than more pixels**, real arrowhead annotations instead of a square at a waypoint. For 9.1.1's open question: its stated reversal threshold of RT-DETR's 0.4863 **is cleared at 0.5197 but by 9.1.3's schedule rather than by this task**, and the two remain un-budget-matched (RT-DETR had 12 epochs at 896, never 40 at 1280), so the condition as written is not met and **the architecture question stays open at equal budget** rather than being settled for yolov8n | ✅ |
| 9.1.6 | NMS tuning | IoU threshold, class-agnostic vs. per-class | `src/detect/nms.py`: 86,430 candidate boxes from 9.1.3's weights over 308 validation pages, decoded once at `iou=0.95` so every one of the twelve settings is applied to an identical candidate set and the sweep measures suppression rather than inference noise. **Recommended: IoU 0.6, per-class** - two of three criteria choose it, mAP@0.5:0.95 dissents for 0.7, and that criterion's spread across the whole grid is **0.0045**, which is not a preference; ultralytics' default of 0.7 costs 0.0023. **Suppression is an arrowhead knob and almost nothing else**: across the grid the pooled figure moves **0.0230** while `arrowhead` moves **0.1760** (0.3627 at IoU 0.9 to 0.5387 at 0.6), eight times as far, because heads cluster densely along a polyline and are the only class where several true instances sit within an IoU of each other and a threshold can therefore destroy them. **That 0.5387 is above 9.1.4's 0.5197 at the default threshold, so setting one number correctly bought more arrowhead AP than 9.1.5's tiling did at 16.5x the latency.** **The prediction this task was built on is wrong, and the counter added to check it is what proved it.** The design argued that a BPMN pool is a `rectangle` containing every task in its lane, so class-agnostic suppression should eat the containment structure Phase 10 depends on; instead **`rectangle` costs exactly 0.0000 and only 5 nested pairs are lost out of 2,744**. The error is a confusion between containment and IoU, worth stating because it is easy to make: a task inside a pool has intersection equal to the task and union equal to the pool, so its IoU with its container is `task_area / pool_area` - **nesting produces low IoU, not high IoU, and NMS never sees it**. The nesting counter was built to quantify damage and instead demonstrated there is none, which is the more useful outcome and the argument for computing a number rather than reasoning about it. **Class-agnostic still loses uniformly - 0.0181 mAP@0.5 at every threshold - and the per-class decomposition shows the loss is not where the pooled figure implies**: `parallelogram` -0.1155 (on **7 instances**, so one box), `double-circle` -0.0198, `circle` -0.0094, everything else 0.0000 +/- 0.0004, which accounts for the whole 0.0181 over eight classes. So **four fifths of class-agnostic's apparent cost is a single box in a 7-instance class**, and the defensible part is the 0.0292 across the two concentric classes - the predicted mechanism in an unpredicted place, since **a double-circle is a ring inside a ring of a different label, two boxes with genuinely high IoU, and agnostic suppression deletes one**. The recommendation is per-class, and the honest reason is not that it protects BPMN pools - nothing threatened those - but that **it protects concentric shapes**, a small effect on a 74-instance class here and a large one on a state-machine corpus, where `double-circle` is how an accepting state is drawn and 2.1.4 froze it as its own shape for that reason | ✅ |
| 9.1.7 | Hard-negative mining | Crossed-out elements, doodles, margin scribbles | `src/detect/negatives.py`: the plan names three things to mine and **none is annotated anywhere in the corpus**, so both available routes are taken - mining the detector's own confident false positives, and synthesizing strike-outs, doodles and margin marks - and three arms are trained 40 epochs at 1280 so the only variable is the data: baseline 0.9210 / 46 val false positives, **mined-only 0.9193 / 36**, mined+synthesized **0.9303 / 45** (all three pre-rebuild: the arms are only comparable to each other, and re-running one of them alone would break that - 9.1.4's rebuilt baseline reads 0.9107). **Mining found almost nothing to mine, which is the first result**: over 1,842 training pages the detector produced **65 confident false positives, 0.035 a page**, and **61 of the 65 are on hdbpmn photographs against 4 on 1,187 rendered pages** - hallucination is a hand-drawn phenomenon and it is rare. They are not tiny (median short side 42 px) and average 0.70 confidence, split `rectangle` 32 / `circle` 17 / `freeform` 7 / `diamond` 6 / `rounded-rect` 3. **The finding is that the two interventions do opposite things, and it exists only because the mined-only control was run.** The mined negatives do exactly what they were added for and nothing else - **false positives 46 -> 36, a 22% reduction, with mAP@0.5 moving -0.0017**, since 59 crops cannot teach a detector to see better but can teach it to stop seeing one particular kind of nothing. The synthesized damage does the opposite: 640 struck-out pages and 1,913 doodles lift mAP@0.5 by **+0.0093** and lift false positives back to 45, **undoing the mining's entire benefit**. So the combined arm - the one the plan's design implies - buys accuracy and loses the false-positive reduction, and its +0.0093 headline would have been attributed to hard negatives by anyone reading it alone; **nothing about hard-negative mining is visible in that number**. 7.4.7, 8.7 and 9.1.5 each needed a control to separate an effect from its confound and this is the fourth. **Why synthesis restores the false positives is offered as a hypothesis rather than a measurement**: the strike-out rule deletes a real node's box while leaving its damaged ink, so **640 genuine shapes are relabelled background**, and that deliberate label noise is the likeliest reason the model becomes more liberal elsewhere - consistent with the numbers, not established by them, and separating "doodles only" from "strike-outs only" would cost two more 40-epoch runs. The accuracy gain is best read as data augmentation, and **7.1.6 measured the same property from the other direction** when its classifier lost only 0.018 macro F1 with half the page stained. **The recommendation for Phase 13 is to ship the mined negatives and not the synthesized damage**: mining is the intervention that reduces the failure a user actually sees - an invented box in the assembled graph - and it costs 59 crops and no accuracy. The Definition of Done is met by all three arms; the useful reading is which of them should be | ✅ |

### 9.2 CNN Fundamentals Deliverable (exam-facing)

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 9.2.1 | Scratch CNN | Hand-built conv/pool/FC classifier for shape crops | `src/detect/crops.py` -> `data/processed/shape_crops` + `src/detect/scratchcnn.py`: **41,014 64x64 greyscale crops** over three sources, split-consistent with 9.1.2 so a page whose boxes trained the detector cannot supply a crop that validates the classifier, and a hand-built six-conv/four-pool/two-FC stack of **372,183 parameters** trained 30 epochs on 33,312 of them. **Macro F1 0.9595 pooled and 0.9840 on the hand-drawn hdbpmn crops**, against a majority baseline of 0.5076; accuracy is 0.9931 and is quoted only to be discounted, since the corpus is 59% `rectangle` and accuracy is the number that would look good regardless - the loss is class-weighted for the same reason. **The finding worth carrying is what this says about 7.4.** 7.4.8 asked exactly this question on exactly this source and reported that **7.4.1's 22 descriptors support a 0.8160 macro F1 ceiling** with a supervised random forest, that the learned GMM vocabulary reached 0.4160 and 4.1.3's template rules 0.3734, and that neither should ship. A CNN reading raw pixels reaches **0.9840 on the same source**, and it is most decisively better exactly where the descriptors were weakest: **`freeform` goes 0.5666 -> 0.9617**, and 7.4.8's own diagnosis was that freeform "is separable in this table, just not by a rule or by a cluster centre, because what identifies it is a conjunction rather than a region" - **a conjunction of features over a region is what a convolutional stack computes**, so the +0.395 there is the clearest single statement in this project that handcrafted descriptors were the binding constraint rather than the difficulty of the task. The comparison is **not controlled and is not offered as one**: 7.4.8 used five page-grouped folds over all 12,400 hdbpmn shapes with four classes, this a writer-disjoint split with five, and this crop carries a 12% context margin a descriptor vector does not - the gap survives those differences, the exact number does not. **Every off-diagonal entry was predicted by an earlier phase**: six of 74 `double-circle` crops go to `circle` when the outer ring is lost to the resize, which is why 2.1.4 froze `double-circle` as its own shape and why the margin exists; `parallelogram` sits at 0.8235 on **7 validation instances** at perfect recall with 3 false positives, so its F1 is a precision artefact of a class too small to measure; `freeform` loses two crops to `rounded-rect`, a BPMN data object drawn with soft corners. **One number is deliberately not a headline** - flowchartseg's crops score 0.999 accuracy because they are computer-rendered with exact boundaries and the pooled figure is 54% those, so the hdbpmn row is the deployment one exactly as in 9.1.4. Training time of 136 s is an upper bound rather than a benchmark, having been measured while a 9.1.7 run held the same card | ✅ |
| 9.2.2 | **Parameter calculation** | Layer-by-layer parameter count and output-size arithmetic table | `src/detect/cnnmath.py` -> `docs/cnn_math.md`: a 27-row table computed from `Spec.layers()` rather than read off a `summary()` call, and **asserted against the real torch module - hand arithmetic and `torch` agree exactly at 372,183**, for the reference architecture and for every configuration 9.2.5 will ablate (5x5 kernels, BatchNorm off, average pooling, a thinner stack, a smaller head). The agreement is the point: a parameter table nobody checked describes a different network than the one that trained. **The finding is that 70.7% of the parameters sit in one layer that does no convolving.** The six convolutions hold 108,432 weights between them and `fc 2048->128` alone holds **262,272**, so the entire feature extractor - four stages, six layers - costs 40% of what the first dense layer costs, with `batchnorm` at 576 a rounding error. That is structural rather than a bad choice: **convolution weights are shared across every position** so a 3x3 layer costs `C_out * C_in * 9` regardless of map size, while a dense layer pays per position - 128 x 4 x 4 = 2,048 inputs each with a weight to each of 128 units - which is why global average pooling replaced flatten-then-dense after 2014, and the table prices what that swap would save here (~110,000 parameters). **The arithmetic also prices the architecture's founding substitution**: two stacked 3x3 layers reach a 5x5 receptive field for `2 x 9 = 18` weights a channel pair where one 5x5 needs 25, a 28% saving with a nonlinearity gained, and across the whole stack `kernel=5` costs exactly 25/9 of `kernel=3`'s convolutional parameters - a ratio the test suite pins and 9.2.5 measures the accuracy consequence of. **One conversion is called out because it is easy to get wrong**: BatchNorm carries `2 * C` learned parameters *and* `2 * C` running buffers, the buffers are not parameters, and counting a `state_dict()` with `numel()` would report 1,152 here instead of 576 while silently disagreeing with `parameters()`; relatedly a convolution before a BatchNorm carries no bias since the BN shift subsumes it, which is why row 1 is 144 and not 160 | ✅ |
| 9.2.3 | Receptive field analysis | RF computed per layer | Same module and same table, propagated forward with `j_out = j_in * s` and `r_out = r_in + (k - 1) * j_in` from `r = 1, j = 1`, with pooling counted (a 2x2 stride-2 pool adds `1 * j` and doubles the jump). The column runs 3, 5, 6, 10, 14, 16, 24, 28, 44 to a final **52**, and the jump doubles at every pool and nowhere else - 2, 4, 8, 16 - which is the tell that stride lives only in the pooling here. **The finding is that no convolutional unit ever sees the whole crop: 52x52 on a 64x64 input, 12 pixels short, so a unit in the last feature map is blind to a 6-pixel border all the way round.** That matters because **shape is a global property** - a rectangle is not identifiable from any 20x20 window of itself, and neither is the difference between a `circle` and a `double-circle`, whose distinguishing outer ring is precisely what sits nearest the crop edge. **Set beside 9.2.2 the two findings are one finding seen twice: whatever integrates the crop into a shape is the fully connected layer, and that is why it holds 70% of the parameters.** The convolutions build local edge and corner evidence with a 52-pixel horizon; the dense layer is the only component that sees all sixteen spatial positions at once and can learn "corners at the four extremes" or "one closed curve inside another", so the global-average-pooling design 9.2.2 prices as a 110,000-parameter saving would lose exactly that ability rather than merely shrinking the model. The stacked-kernel claim is checked rather than asserted - rows 1 and 4 both arrive at RF 5, so two 3x3 layers do reach what one 5x5 does - and a wider kernel widens the final field monotonically (3 < 5 < 7), which is the knob 9.2.5 turns | ✅ |
| 9.2.4 | Filter / feature-map visualization | First-layer filters + activations on a real sketch | `src/detect/visualise.py` -> `reports/figures/p9_filters.png` + `p9_featuremaps.png`: the 16 first-layer 3x3 kernels and the 16 activations they produce, on a **real validation crop** rather than a synthetic square, since the question is what the network learned from photographed pen strokes. **The deliverable beside the pictures is the number that makes them checkable**: sixteen 3x3 kernels drawn as small red-blue squares are close to unreadable and every such figure ever published looks equally convincing, so the title carries the **mean absolute cosine similarity between distinct kernels, 0.2852** (max 0.8233, weight sd 0.2028, norms 0.4823-0.7342). Near 1.0 would mean the layer learned one filter sixteen times - the failure a filter grid exists to reveal and almost never does - and at 0.285 the layer is genuinely diverse, with a single pair at 0.82 as the residual redundancy. The figure is produced for the reference network of 9.2.1 and is regenerated by the same command that writes 9.2.6's heatmaps, so the two cannot come to describe different weights | ✅ |
| 9.2.5 | Ablations | Kernel size, depth, pooling type, BatchNorm on/off | `src/detect/ablations.py`: seven arms at 20 epochs, each **one knob from the reference** so every row is attributable, with the parameter count printed beside every score because an ablation that moves accuracy and parameters together has isolated nothing. Reference 0.9511; `kernel_5` 0.9481 (564,951 params), `kernel_7` 0.9519 (854,103), `depth_3` 0.9344 (560,343), `depth_2` 0.9430 (1,066,071), `pool_avg` 0.9463 (372,183), `no_batchnorm` **0.8990** (371,895). **BatchNorm is the only knob that matters and it is free: removing it costs 0.0521 macro F1 - ten times the next largest effect - while changing the parameter count by 288, or 0.08%.** Every other axis moves the score under 0.017 and the three that move it most cost 1.5x-2.9x the parameters to do it; in one sentence, **the architecture was not the problem, the normalisation was the only thing holding it up**. **Enlarging the kernel buys nothing, which directly refutes the obvious reading of 9.2.3**: that task found the field 12 px short of the input and noted shape is a global property, so widening it should help - but `kernel_5` reaches RF 88 and `kernel_7` RF 124, **both comfortably covering the 64-pixel crop**, and they score -0.0030 and +0.0008 for 1.5x and 2.3x the weights. Covering the input is worth nothing measurable because 9.2.3's other half was already the explanation: **the fully connected layer does the global integration whether or not the convolutions could have**, and `kernel_7`'s +0.0008 is inside run noise. **Depth is the axis where "fewer parameters" would have been the wrong summary**: removing stages *increases* the count - 4 stages 372k, 3 stages 560k, 2 stages 1.07M - because each dropped pool doubles the flatten width and the first dense layer pays per position, so `depth_2` is a **2.9x larger model scoring 0.0081 worse** and `depth_3` is 1.5x larger and 0.0167 worse. **Parameters and capacity are not the same quantity**, and the pooling that removes parameters is what buys the depth that earns the accuracy. Average pooling costs 0.0048 at identical cost, consistent with a domain of thin dark strokes on white paper where max-pooling propagates the strongest local response and averaging dilutes a 1-pixel stroke over a 2x2 window. **The table's whole spread is 0.0529 and 0.0521 of it is BatchNorm** - excluding that row every architectural choice here is worth under 0.017, the same shape as 9.1.1's three detector families inside 0.014 and 6.3.7's six classifiers inside 0.012: three phases, three model families, one conclusion | ✅ |
| 9.2.6 | Grad-CAM | What the network looks at per shape class | Same module -> `reports/figures/p9_gradcam.png`: gradients of each class logit against the last convolutional map, average-pooled into channel weights and ReLU'd, one panel per class. **The finding is that the class evidence is mostly not on the ink, and which classes are the exception is the result.** Measured over **290 validation crops** rather than the seven on the figure, and reported as *enrichment* - Grad-CAM mass on ink divided by the share of pixels that are ink - so a crop being mostly paper cannot masquerade as a finding: `freeform` **1.117** (60% of crops above 1.0), `double-circle` **1.104** (74%), `rounded-rect` 0.753, `diamond` 0.410, `parallelogram` 0.031, `rectangle` **0.000**, `circle` **0.000**, **median across classes 0.41 - the heat sits on paper at less than half of chance**. And the two classes above 1.0 are exactly the two whose identity *is* a local ink feature: a `double-circle` is defined by its second ring and a `freeform` by an irregular boundary, and both require looking at a stroke. The classes at zero are those whose identity is the **shape of the enclosed region** - what makes a rectangle is where its area extends, not what its outline is made of, which at the last conv layer is carried by which of the 8x8 cells are active rather than by whether they sit on a line. **Read with 9.2.3 this is coherent rather than surprising**: the final receptive field is 52 of 64 px and the fully connected layer holds 70% of the parameters, so **the global shape reasoning happens after the last convolution, in a layer with no spatial extent that Grad-CAM cannot see at all** - a heatmap here shows where the *local* evidence was gathered, and for four of seven classes that is honestly not on the strokes. Three limits are recorded rather than smoothed over: the map is **8x8 upsampled to 64x64** so every claim has a resolution of 8 pixels and the figure says so; **17 of 307 crops have an empty ink mask at a 0.5 grey threshold** (faint pencil) and are excluded rather than scored zero, that being a limit of the mask and not evidence about the model; and `parallelogram`'s row rests on the same **7 instances** that make its F1 unreadable everywhere else in 9.2 | ✅ |
| 9.2.7 | Transfer vs. scratch | Quantify the pretraining benefit | `src/detect/transfer.py`: four arms at 20 epochs on identical crops - scratch **0.9574**, `resnet18_ft` 0.9475, `resnet18_random` 0.9440, `resnet18_frozen` **0.7928**. **"Transfer beats scratch" is false here, and the fourth arm is what turns that from an anecdote into a decomposition.** The naive comparison reads **-0.0099** - a fine-tuned ImageNet ResNet *loses* to a hand-built network with **30x fewer parameters** - and splitting it gives **pretraining benefit = ft - random = +0.0035** and **architecture benefit = random - scratch = -0.0134**. So **ImageNet pretraining is worth three thousandths of macro F1, indistinguishable from run noise, and the larger architecture is worth minus 0.013.** Without the random-init ResNet the only available reading would have been "transfer loses by 0.01" with no way to attribute it; the answer is that the weights help imperceptibly and the architecture hurts - the same shape 7.4.7 found pricing per-scribe adaptation and 8.7 pricing per-style OCR heads, three tasks agreeing that **on this corpus the bigger or more specialised model loses to the smaller general one**. **Freezing is a disaster and the most informative arm**: 0.7928, a cost of **0.1547** against fine-tuning with only 3,591 trainable parameters, and the per-class breakdown names exactly which features are missing - `parallelogram` **0.2500**, `freeform` 0.7489, `double-circle` 0.7543, against `diamond` 0.9847 and `rectangle` 0.9676. **The classes it can still do are the ones with strong oriented edges** - the one thing ImageNet's early layers genuinely transfer - **and the ones it cannot are those needing a shape-specific conjunction**: a slanted pair of sides, an irregular outline, one ring inside another. That is the domain gap the task predicted in advance, measured rather than asserted. What fine-tuning amounts to here is **a marginally better initialisation rather than reusable features**, since 33,312 in-domain crops leave pretraining little to add and the frozen arm shows what happens when it is forced to supply everything. **The recommendation is the scratch network** - best macro F1, 30x smaller, 48 s against 64 s - and the single number favouring the ResNets is accuracy (0.9927 against 0.9922), which is the 59%-`rectangle` imbalance talking and precisely why macro F1 is the reported metric | ✅ |

### 9.3 Handwriting OCR Sub-Pipeline

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 9.3.1 | Text-crop extraction | Crop text regions from detected components and edges | `src/ocr/textcrops.py` -> `data/processed/text_crops`: **25,814 crops over 993 pages and 130 writers**, 21,912 hdbpmn and 3,902 fa_bresler, split 16,974/4,530/4,310 under 1.3.3's writer-disjoint assignment; 5,218 distinct strings, median length 14, alphabet 85. Three provenances, because the plan's phrase *from detected components* is a different corpus from the one 8.7 built: **annotated 11,261** (a human's node box, inset 10%), **derived 4,864** (an edge label), **detected 9,689** (9.1.3's own box, matched to a node at IoU >= 0.5). **The headline is that there is no text-region annotation anywhere in this project** - 9.1.2 found it from the detection side, and here it means every crop is a derivation, of which the edge-label rule is by far the weaker: an edge carries only a polyline, so the box is a rectangle at the arc-length midpoint sized from the page's median glyph height. **That rule misses the writing one time in five.** 80.59% of edge crops contain ink that is not the connector they were centred on, against **99.72%** for annotated node crops - a gap of **0.1913**, so about **944 edge crops are a picture of a line with no label in it**, carrying a transcription no recogniser can earn and setting a floor under every edge CER in 9.3.2-9.3.6. **The first version of that check scored 0.9979 and was worthless**, because a box centred on a polyline contains the polyline by construction - it measured that the rule had found the line, which was never in doubt; rasterising the polyline, dilating it and removing it is what turns the test into one the rule can fail, and an even-sided dilation kernel that left one row of connector standing was itself worth 1.4 points of the rate. **The detector turns out to be a good cropper**: median IoU **0.9042** against the node it matched and **9,689 of 11,261 text-bearing nodes recovered (86.0%)**, which splits 9.3.6's deployment gap into two separately reportable parts - 14% of labels get no crop at all, and the 86% that do get one whose box is 0.90 rather than 1.00 IoU, the second being much the smaller problem. **Edge labels are a third of the corpus and were entirely absent from 8.7**, and their text is nothing like the nodes': `a`, `b`, `0` and `1` are the four most frequent strings in the whole corpus, which is why 9.3.4 refuses to snap words shorter than three characters | ✅ |
| 9.3.2 | CRNN + CTC | CNN encoder → BiLSTM → CTC decoder; IAM pretrain, diagram-label fine-tune | `src/ocr/crnn.py`: six conv blocks (the last four pooling height only, because **width is the time axis and every column removed is a character CTC can no longer place**) into a 2-layer BiLSTM and a CTC head, 3,787,806 parameters, alphabet 61. Five arms - `iam` **0.1157** CER / `zero_shot` 0.9338 / `scratch` 0.8397 / `finetune` **0.6864** / `unwrap` 0.6889. **The IAM row is the control that makes the others interpretable and it is the finding: the same network, trained the same way on the same GPU, reads real cursive English at CER 0.1157 - inside 9.3.6's 0.15 target - and reads this project's diagram labels six times worse.** So the failure is not the architecture, the alphabet, the CTC loss or the optimiser; **it is the crops** - an IAM line is one tightly-cropped line on a ruled page, a 9.3.1 node crop is a phone photograph of two or three lines of block capitals stacked inside a hand-drawn box whose border is a tenth of the way in from every edge. **Pretraining is worth 0.1533 CER, 22.4% relative - 44 times what 9.2.7 measured** for ImageNet on shape crops (+0.0035 macro F1, noise) - and the docstring predicted the difference in advance, because this is the same experiment with the domain gap closed: handwriting to handwriting rather than natural photographs to line drawings. **It is the first place in this project where transfer has clearly paid, and it pays for the reason the theory says it should.** The `scratch` loss curve shows why: 5.79 -> 3.23 in one epoch and then a crawl to 2.42, with degenerate outputs (`evaluate application`, `write notification email` and `rejected` all decode to `ce e` or `co`) - it is stuck in the blank optimum, and IAM's real contribution is an initialisation from which the blank collapse is not the easiest move. **The `unwrap` arm was this task's own hypothesis and it is refuted**: CTC pools height to nothing, so a label whose second word sits *below* the first is stacked along the axis the model destroys, and cutting the crop into ink bands and laying them end to end should fix it - it scores **0.6889 against 0.6864, no difference**, 0.0025 the wrong way, with a loss curve trailing `finetune` by 0.04 at every epoch. Wrapping is not the bottleneck; resolution, the drawn border inside the crop and 105 different phones are what is left | ✅ |
| 9.3.3 | TrOCR comparison | Fine-tune a transformer OCR baseline; compare CER/WER | `src/ocr/trocr.py`: `trocr-small-handwritten`, 61,596,672 parameters, on identical crops with 9.3.6's evaluator. CRNN 0.6864 / **TrOCR fine-tuned 0.8211** / TrOCR zero-shot 1.1702. **The transformer loses by 0.1347 CER with 16.3x the parameters** - the fourth time in this project that the smaller model has won a like-for-like comparison (7.4.7, 8.7, 9.2.7). **The length breakdown is the finding and it confirms the predicted mechanism**: by label length, CRNN scores 0.6916 / 0.6545 / 0.6855 on the 13+, 4-12 and 1-3 character bands - **flat** - while TrOCR scores 0.7532 / 1.1733 / **1.4052**, doubling its error as labels get shorter. A CER above 1.0 means more wrong characters emitted than the truth contains, which is exactly what an autoregressive decoder does when asked for `a` or `q0`: it has a language model, the language model wants a sentence, and nothing in the architecture can decline. **CTC cannot hallucinate because it has nothing to condition on, and on those 672 short crops that is worth 0.72 CER.** 8.7 saw this zero-shot; here it **survives four epochs of fine-tuning**. **The prediction was half wrong**: TrOCR was expected to *win* on long phrases and it loses there too (0.7532 against 0.6916), so the language model is not paying for itself anywhere on this corpus. Where it does win is **exact match, 0.0704 against 0.0660** - and per band it wins the two long ones (0.0221 vs 0.0018, 0.0813 vs 0.0189) and loses the short one badly (0.2545 vs 0.4077), so the decoder does produce completely correct *long* labels more often while being worse on characters. **The fairness caveat is stated rather than buried**: TrOCR had 4 epochs to the CRNN's 30 and its loss was still falling (2.95, 2.14, 1.90, 1.75), because a TrOCR epoch costs 250 s against 19 s - so this is equal wall-clock, not equal epochs. That does not touch the length result: no further training makes an autoregressive decoder willing to emit two characters. Inference moves the interesting way - **95.58 ms/crop zero-shot against 11.91 ms fine-tuned**, an 8x speedup from the same weights, because generation stops at the end token and fine-tuning taught it these labels are short; the 95.58 ms is the model writing prose nobody asked for, timed | ✅ |
| 9.3.4 | Domain lexicon biasing | Constrain decoding with a diagram vocabulary (`if`, `else`, `start`, `end`, `user_id`, `submit`, …) | `src/ocr/lexicon.py`: CTC prefix beam search (the two-probability blank/non-blank recursion, because `aa` and `a-a` collapse differently and one probability per prefix cannot express that) with word-level snapping under a length-scaled edit budget. 2,811 val crops, vocabulary 4,149 entries from the *training* split only, OOV **0.0281**. greedy 0.6972 / beam 0.6839 / domain 0.6865 / generic 0.7171. **CER improves by 0.0107 against 9.3.2's decode, which meets the row - and most of it is the beam, not the lexicon.** Against the unconstrained beam the vocabulary actually *costs* 0.0026 CER; without that control the +0.0107 would have been reported as the vocabulary's contribution and it is mostly not. **The lexicon's real effect is on words and it is large**: WER **0.9438 -> 0.8907** and exact match **0.1046 -> 0.1185, +13% relative**, while CER gets slightly worse. That is what snapping is - replacing `subrnit` with `submit` fixes a word and one character, replacing it with `summit` costs two characters and fixes nothing - so **a lexicon trades characters for words**, converting near-misses into exact hits or confident misses, and Phase 12 needs an identifier rather than a nearly-correct string. **The `generic` control is what makes this a measurement**: a word list of exactly the same size drawn from IAM prose and disjoint from the diagram vocabulary makes everything worse than not snapping at all (CER 0.7171, WER 0.9911, exact 0.0928), so this vocabulary is worth **0.0306 CER and 0.1004 WER over an arbitrary one** and the deflationary reading *words beat non-words* is ruled out. **OOV is not the limitation** at 2.81%; what limits it is that at CER 0.68 the beam's output is often too far from any real word for the budget to reach, and widening the budget would snap unrelated words together. A hard trie constraint during the beam is deliberately not used: it cannot represent a word the vocabulary lacks, so it converts a partially correct read into a confidently wrong one, where a budgeted snap degrades to the unconstrained answer | ✅ |
| 9.3.5 | Per-style adaptation hook | Consume Phase 8 style clusters | `src/ocr/adapt.py`: 8.6's three clusters consumed the way **8.7's own closing paragraph said they should have been** - per-cluster *adaptation from the global head* rather than independent fine-tuning - so each head inherits all 16,974 training crops before specialising. 4,530 val crops, 86.09% routable (fa_bresler's writers have no 8.6 style and fall back to the global head, so every arm is scored on identical crops). `style_adapt` **0.6816** / `random_adapt` 0.6818 / `global` 0.6864. **8.7's diagnosis was half right and this is the half that was**: 8.7 measured per-style heads costing **+31.3% relative CER**, and removing the arithmetic penalty turns that into **-0.70%** - a small improvement instead of a large regression - which confirms that the 23.9% its `random` arm attributed to *dividing the training set* was correctly attributed, because it vanishes exactly when the division does. **The other half survives untouched and it decides the task**: three heads adapted on random writer groups of the same sizes score 0.6818 against style_adapt's 0.6816, so **8.6's clustering contributes 0.0002 CER** - two ten-thousandths, not a result. 8.7 found the clusters 6.0% *worse* than random; with the penalty gone they are neither better nor worse, and the whole 0.0048 gain is explained by *a few more epochs on a subset*, which an arbitrary subset delivers just as well. **The hook is built, integrated and measured, and the recommendation is not to route.** The mechanism is 8.7's: the split is by writer, so every test hand is unseen, and adaptation narrows a head *away* from it - less far than independent fine-tuning did, which is why the penalty shrank, and in the same direction, which is why the clustering earns nothing. **Three phases have now asked this corpus to support a specialised model - 7.4.7's per-scribe densities, 8.7's per-style heads, and this - and the answer has been the same every time, for the same reason** | ✅ |
| 9.3.6 | Metrics | CER, WER overall and per style cluster | `src/ocr/metrics.py` -> `reports/ocr.md`: one evaluator for all of 9.3, thirteen runs scored through it. **The target is CER <= 0.15 and nothing comes close - the best model in Phase 9.3 is 0.6816** (`adapt_style_adapt`), with `crnn_finetune` 0.6864, `trocr_finetune` 0.8211 and `trocr_zero_shot` 1.1702. **The spread among the seven serious arms is 0.0355 CER and the distance from all of them to the target is 0.53** - the same shape as 9.1.1's three detector families inside 0.014 and 9.2.5's seven ablations inside 0.017: **the choices being compared are much smaller than the thing that is wrong**, which 9.3.2 identified as the crops rather than the model. **The per-provenance row is the deployment answer and it is good news: cropping from 9.1.3's detector instead of a human's box costs 0.0040 CER (0.6690 -> 0.6730)**, nothing beside the 0.53 gap, so with 9.3.1's 86.0% recall at median IoU 0.9042 **the detector is not the bottleneck and need not improve before OCR does** - though exact match falls 0.0739 -> 0.0070, a factor of ten, because box jitter destroys *completely correct* reads while barely moving the character rate. **The per-kind row prices 9.3.1's edge rule exactly**: edge crops 0.8332 against nodes' 0.6709, predicted in advance by the 19.4% of derived edge boxes containing no ink but the connector, so **a sixth of the edge CER is a cropping rule and not a recogniser** (their exact-match is nonetheless the highest in the table, 0.1705, because correctly-cropped edge labels are single characters). Per source, **fa_bresler 0.4788 at exact match 0.4429 against hdbpmn 0.6896 at 0.0051** - single rendered alphabet symbols versus wrapped English phrases photographed on paper, **two different tasks sharing one metric**, which is why the breakdown exists. **Per style cluster there is nothing there**: 0.6935 / 0.6933 / 0.6865, a spread of **0.0070** over 3,900 hdbpmn crops, which independently confirms 9.3.5 without training anything - a per-style head could only help if the per-style error differed first, and it does not. The two CER definitions disagree by up to 0.07 here (`trocr_zero_shot` 1.1702 corpus-level against 0.8773 as a capped mean of per-crop rates), which is why both columns are printed: the mean-of-rates form would rank zero-shot TrOCR above `crnn_scratch`, and the corpus form the target is written against says it is far worse | ✅ |
| 9.3.7 | Confidence + fallback | Low-confidence labels flagged for user correction in the UI | `src/ocr/confidence.py` -> `low_conf_text` on 125 hdbpmn pages, **467 nodes flagged**. Three candidate confidences read off the CTC path, scored by AUROC at predicting an incorrect read over 2,811 val crops (89.79% of which are not exactly right): `path_logp` **0.9497**, `min_char` 0.9345, **`length` 0.8880**, `mean_char` 0.8716, `ink` 0.5889, `random` 0.4849. **The length control is the one that matters and the confidence beats it by 0.0617**: label length alone reaches 0.888 on a corpus mixing `q0` with 28-character BPMN phrases, so a score that had merely learned *long labels are wrong* would have landed there - the posterior carries 0.06 AUROC about *this image* beyond what length says. On the number the UI acts on, flagging the least-confident 20% captures **34.44% of all character error, lift 1.72**, against 27.23% and 1.36 for length. **`mean_char` is the worst of the three model scores and that is the informative part**: averaging over emitting columns forgives the one bad glyph that makes a label wrong, so it ranks a long read with one hopeless character above a short uncertain one - backwards for a whole-string decision - and the pessimistic `min_char` beats it by 0.0629. **Calibration is a disaster and is separate from the ranking**: ECE **0.8121**, with the bins from confidence 0.80 to 0.92 - 1,687 crops, 60% of the corpus - containing **not one exactly correct read**, and the top bin at 0.9992 right only 58.36% of the time. CTC is confident about *paths* and nothing in its training asks path confidence to agree with label correctness, so **the number is a rank and never a probability**; Phase 16 must not render it as *97% sure*, and a threshold chosen at a review budget is the correct consumption because a budget needs only the ordering. **Getting the flags into the IR took two bugs and both are the same mistake**: the first `annotate` wrote to `<page>.json` where the files are `<page>.ir.json`, silently annotating zero pages while reporting 467 flags (`pages_not_found` is now returned beside `pages_annotated`, because a writer that finds nothing to write to must say so rather than report success); the second wrote bare node-id strings where 2.1's schema requires `{ref, confidence, note}` objects - which round-trips through `json` perfectly, passes every test in this module, and breaks `Diagram.from_dict` on the next load, where it was caught by `tests/test_preprocess_page.py` three commits later and nowhere near here. **The IR is a schema, not a dictionary that happens to be JSON**: a module writing into shared state has to be validated against the consumer's loader and not against its own round trip, and `annotate` now also drops any `ref` that does not name a node on the page, which is the invariant `src/ir/qa.py` checks | ✅ |
| 9.3.8 | Crossed-out detection | Classifier for struck-through / scribbled-out elements → excluded from IR | `src/ocr/strikeout.py` -> `data/processed/strikeout`: 33,687 crops, 11,229 each of `clean`, `struck` and `inked`, split 22,080/5,955/5,652 writer-disjoint. **Precision 0.9990 at recall 1.0000 against the 0.85 target - and the number is worth almost nothing.** The first finding is that **not one page in this project is annotated with a struck-out element**: `crossed_out` is empty in all 5,796 IR files, because hdbpmn's transcriptions come from the BPMN model the writer was copying, so anything crossed out never entered the annotation. The positive class is therefore synthesised from 9.1.7's three gestures, and **the control that holds one gesture out of training entirely is what exposes what was really learned: recall collapses from 1.0000 to 0.0163 - 10 of 615 scribbles found** - when the zig-zag is removed from the training set and asked for at validation. The classifier has not learned *a stroke crossing the writing*; it has learned the two gestures it was shown, and a third drawn by the same code with the same pen is invisible to it. If a synthetic gesture it has never seen defeats it, a real one will. **The ink control rules out the cheap explanation and passes**: a third class `inked` adds the same quantity of ink *beside* the text rather than across it (median ink 0.3307 against `struck`'s 0.3122, the control slightly overshooting, which is the safe direction), and a threshold on ink share reaches precision **0.4062** against the 0.333 a positive is worth by chance - so the model is reading geometry, just far narrower geometry than the task wanted. All 2 false positives are `clean` and none is `inked`. **Two earlier versions failed the same way**: the first scored precision exactly 1.000 using a constant grey-20 ink and constant thickness - a watermark rather than a gesture - and drawing the pen from the crop's own 5th percentile barely moved the score, which alone would have read as *the watermark was not being used*; it took the held-out gesture to show something almost as narrow had been learned instead. **A control the model passes tells you much less than one it fails**, and this row has one of each. The recommendation for Phase 16 is not the precision: it is that **crossed-out detection cannot be validated on this corpus at all**, and a real annotation campaign must collect struck-out pages before the flag is trusted | ✅ |

---

# Phase 10 — Graph Assembly & Semantic Structure IR

**Goal:** turn detections into a clean, validated graph — the contract between vision and codegen.

### 10.1 Assembly

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 10.1.1 | Node instantiation | Detections + GMM shape posterior + OCR text → nodes | `src/assemble/nodes.py`: detections become nodes in three cuts, each measured rather than assumed, over the 470 held-out pages. Of **24,385 raw boxes, 66.4% are `arrowhead`** - 9.1.2's derived class, which is not a node - and they are filtered before scoring rather than counted as false positives. The score threshold is swept rather than asserted: pooled strict (IoU ≥ 0.5) **F1 peaks at 0.9837 at `MIN_SCORE=0.30`** (precision 0.9821, recall 0.9853; loose IoU ≥ 0.25 gives 0.9840, so the boxes are being *found* and not merely nearly-found), flat across 0.25-0.35, with hdbpmn recall (0.9819 → 0.9500) the component that erodes fastest as the bar rises. Cross-class de-duplication at IoU ≥ 0.60 - catching what 9.1's *per-class* NMS at 0.7 structurally cannot, since one drawn shape detected as both `rectangle` and `rounded-rect` is two boxes no per-class pass will ever compare - removes 50 real duplicates for 0.0024 F1. **Containment was measured as a second duplication test and rejected on the number**: enabling it drops pooled F1 to **0.8803** and costs hdbpmn alone **0.2019 F1**, because BPMN pools and lanes genuinely nest their children, so a contained box is signal and not noise; `NEST_FRACTION` stays `None`. **The per-source split is the honest reading and the pooled number hides it**: fa_bresler 1.0000 and flowchartseg 0.9997 against hdbpmn's **0.9715**, which is the only source carrying arrowheads (all 16,192) and which drags the pooled average down by itself. **The plan's GMM shape posterior was built, scored and left unwired, which is the finding rather than a gap**: against the detector's own class on 4,249 matched hdbpmn nodes it reaches **0.6293 where the detector reaches 0.9965**, and a confidence-gated fusion (0.9852) is still *worse than doing nothing* - on the 1,572 cases where the two disagree the GMM is right **0.32%** of the time. A refiner that loses to what it refines is not wired in, and `build()` takes the detector's class. Text is deliberately left empty (10.1.2 owns binding). Shape accuracy 0.9970 strict, 0.9980 coarse | ✅ |
| 10.1.2 | Text-to-node binding | Containment, then nearest centroid, then Hungarian assignment | `src/assemble/textbind.py`: the plan's cascade built as three *separately scored* stages, on 470 held-out pages. Ground truth is constructed geometrically - a text box's true owner is the smallest **ground-truth** node containing it, `None` where none does - while the binder only ever sees **detected** boxes and 3.2's text proposals, with its answer mapped back by IoU at scoring time. **Overall accuracy 0.8616 against the 0.90 target: not met**, reported as measured rather than tuned into compliance. **The first thing this row had to do was catch its own stale write-up**, which quoted 22,586 text boxes where the real corpus has **149,547** - a 6.6x gap that turned out to be neither a cache bug nor a partial corpus (the fingerprinted per-page cache is warm and current for all 470 pages) but an earlier, stricter state of 3.2.7's proposer, and the docstring now narrates the old numbers only as contrast. That correction inverts the module's own headline: because the larger proposer finds an owner for most fragments it emits, **owned share flips from 0.291 to 0.7834**, so **"reject everything" collapses from 0.940 to 0.2166 and stops being a credible baseline**, and owner-only accuracy at **0.9139** is now *higher* than overall rather than lower. **The obvious objection was checked rather than waved away**: a BPMN pool is a page-sized rectangle that contains nearly all ink, so `CONTAINER_AREA_FRAC` excludes page-spanning nodes from owner candidacy and re-scores - owned share falls **0.7834 → 0.64**, confirming containers manufacture about fourteen points of it for free, but they account for only **18.3%** of owned boxes, so most of the flip is ordinary small nodes trivially owning the ink drawn inside them, and both container-excluded figures are permanent fields in the output. Stage by stage, **containment resolves 80.5% of the corpus at 0.9223** and the shortfall is the **1,396 owned boxes the cascade never binds at all** - a detection-recall problem, not a cascade-precision one, and no tolerance widening fixes a node that is not in the input. **The plan's Hungarian stage now earns its place, reversing the earlier finding**: 0.8616 against greedy's 0.8093, because the residual is no longer a rounding error on a much smaller corpus. hdbpmn supplies 95.6% of all boxes and therefore sets the headline, while fa_bresler and flowchartseg post >0.99 owner-only accuracy on far sparser proposals. **Wired, and a second binder added for state machines.** This row's cascade is a *geometric* result and nothing consumed it: S5's assembled IR carried no text at all until `src/assemble/statelabels.py`. For state machines the cascade is not needed - the label is inside the circle, so the node box is the crop - and the recogniser was the real blocker: **`labelcrops.build` is hardcoded to hdbpmn, so S3 had never seen an automaton label and scores 1.3% exact on them**. A TrOCR fine-tune on the text-crop corpus's fa_bresler rows reaches **94.4%**, taking fa_bresler's S5 text edits 232 → 15 on val and to **2 across 48 test pages** | ✅ |
| 10.1.3 | Edge tracing | Follow skeleton polylines between node boundaries | `src/assemble/tracing.py`: erase the node boxes, thin what ink is left, walk the skeleton between junctions, chain collinear arms *through* junctions, and attach each end to the nearest box. **Over 470 held-out pages against 4,712 ground-truth edges: recall 0.4675, precision 0.2548, F1 0.3299, 36.74% of ends dangling** (`experiments/assemble/tracing_photo.json`), against the 0.2328 / 0.0626 / 0.0987 this row reported before - **F1 3.3x, and 5.7x on hdbpmn alone (0.0537 -> 0.3062)**. Traced polylines fall from 17,785 to 8,731 against 4,712 real edges. **The previous conclusion is retracted, and the way it was reached is the lesson.** This row read its ceiling run - ground-truth boxes moving recall only 0.2328 -> 0.2455 - as proof that 'the tracer, not 9.1, owns essentially all of the loss'. That inference was blind by construction: **hdBPMN draws pools and lanes and the IR records them as nodes, so the page-spanning container was present in *both* arms**. `box_distance` returns 0 inside a box, so a pool sits at distance 0 from every endpoint and wins every attachment; and erasing it **deleted 100% of ground-truth connector ink on val pages that draw pools** (median surviving edge-ink 0.178 on val, 0.000 on many pages). Swapping perfect boxes for imperfect ones could not move a number that a third box was setting. Excluding boxes above `MAX_BOX_AREA = 0.10` of the page from erasure and attachment is worth roughly 60% of the gain and **costs nothing measurable: 0 of 1,228 train ground-truth edges have an endpoint on a box that large**; the threshold was swept 0.02-0.50 with a flat optimum at 0.06-0.12. Containers stay in the node list, so node recall is untouched. It also explains the 6.7x hdbpmn/fa_bresler gap better than this row's 'density' did - **fa_bresler draws no containers**. The other 40% is binarisation, and it is 3.1.5's caveat coming due rather than a new bug: that module chose Otsu-with-CLAHE on rasterised pen trajectories and wrote down that it had not been measured on photographs. On photographs **CLAHE promotes paper grain to ink** - mean ink fraction 0.0954 where a pen drawing is 2-5% - and dropping it for flat-field division alone is worth +0.046 tracing F1 before any threshold change; `binarize.PHOTO` (sauvola w25 k0.25, flat-field, 2e-4 component floor) lands ink at 0.0314 against a perfect-ink control's 0.0315 and tracing F1 at 0.2467 on the tuning sample. **The 3.1.5 default is deliberately unchanged** - it is still the right answer for the corpus it was measured on, and Phases 3, 4 and 9 are calibrated on it. **All selection ran on 76 train pages sampled every 6th across all eleven exercises; held-out was run once, after freezing.** A first sweep on `pages(splits=('train',))[:60]` was discarded because name-sorting made it ex00 plus part of ex01 - the two exercises *without* pools - and it read recall 0.7746 where the corpus reads 0.1475. **The walk is now genuinely the largest remaining term, and this time it is measured rather than inferred**: rasterising the ground-truth polylines and running the same tracer gives recall 0.7239 / precision 0.6386, while testing those same polylines' endpoints geometrically binds **1,197 of 1,228 (97.5%)** to the correct pair - so ~97% is reachable by geometry, 72% survives the chaining, 37% survives real ink, and the 25-point loss on a perfect mask is the crossing-chaining rule. **One regression is recorded rather than netted away: fa_bresler falls 0.539 -> 0.4124 F1** (recall 0.8825 -> 0.9730, precision 0.3880 -> 0.2616); on *train* fa_bresler the two settings are within noise, so there was no train evidence for a per-source setting and none was made. Rejected on the numbers: morphological closing (0.2075 against 0.2086), a component floor at 5e-4 (eats strokes, 0.2247 -> 0.1915), windows 41 and 61 (within noise of 25), and keeping 3.2.8's text/shape split, which costs 0.038 F1 because the text proposal takes connector ink with it. **A precision defect found by S5 and fixed.** Attaching both ends of a stub to the nearest box produces a self-loop that was never drawn - **80 of fa_bresler's 147 false predicted edges on val**. True and false loops separate on the longest supporting polyline (median **0.166** of the page diagonal against **0.035**), so `s5.MIN_LOOP_LENGTH = 0.072` drops the pair when no trace supports it, removing all 80 for 4 true loops. **Not source-specific: hdbpmn's edge inserts fall 740 → 343 on the same change.** What remains on hdbpmn is recall, not precision - 1,251 edge deletes on test against this row's own 0.7239 perfect-ink ceiling | ✅ |
| 10.1.4 | **Broken-arrow repair** | Gap bridging within tolerance, direction continuity, endpoint snapping to nearest node port | `src/assemble/bridging.py`: built on **the stub 7.3.10 said this task needed rather than the deleted edge its own damage model destroyed**. Cutting each eligible polyline at a random interior gap leaves two stubs that keep their endpoints, direction and length - the evidence 7.3.10 explicitly recorded as missing - and the recovery curve, not a single number, is the result, because *"80% recovered"* is meaningless without saying how broken the input was. Sized as a share of the **page diagonal**: recovery holds above **0.99 to a 2% gap**, **0.9196 at 8%**, and clears the target at **0.8179 recovery / 0.8228 precision at 12%**, falling below it only at 20% (0.6885 / 0.7133). Sized instead as a share of **each edge's own length**, every point from 10% to 70% clears 80% (0.9859 down to 0.820), and both sweeps are reported because the diagonal framing silently erases short edges while the length framing never does. **Against 7.3.10's ceiling of 5.3% recovery at 5.8% precision this is not a marginal improvement but a change of kind**, and it is the direct confirmation of that row's diagnosis: giving the repairer geometry it can interrogate - *does this stub point at that one* - is the difference between failing on purpose and clearing the target. **Precision is tracked at every operating point for the reason 7.3.10 established**, that a repairer inventing seventeen wrong edges per right one is worse than none: here precision tracks recovery within half a point everywhere except the widest gaps, where it runs slightly *ahead* of it. The three-cue ablation at an 8% gap over 1,904 broken edges locates the work exactly: **distance alone scores 0.749, identical to the unconditional-nearest-pair control, so the tolerance gate discriminates nothing by itself**; **direction continuity is what carries the row, lifting it to 0.9196 (+17.1 points over the control)**; and port snapping, though it has real signal alone (0.879, +13.0), is noisier, so **folding all three in at equal weight scores 0.898 - worse than direction alone** - and the shipped default is therefore distance-gated and direction-ranked with `WEIGHTS["port"] = 0`, the cue left wired in behind `use_port=` for re-checking rather than deleted. **The honest limit is that this measures repair of synthetic gaps in ground-truth polylines, not of the fragments 10.1.3 actually produces**, where tracing recall is 0.2328 | ✅ |
| 10.1.5 | Arrow direction resolution | Arrowhead evidence + flow-direction prior + HMM role constraints | `src/assemble/direction.py`: five antisymmetric features (`prior_x`, `prior_y`, `head_det`, `head_geo`, `role`) combined by a no-intercept log-odds fit on **val and reported on test**, scored on ground-truth polylines with ground-truth endpoints so direction error is isolated from tracing error, and ablated by evidence source rather than quoted as one number. **The target is met on hdbpmn at 0.9108 and missed on fa_bresler at 0.7611, and the two must not be pooled.** `head_det` - 9.1's learned `arrowhead` class - carries nearly all of hdbpmn's result (**0.8803 alone**), and fa_bresler has **zero** `head_det` signal because 9.1.2 only ever derived arrowhead boxes on hdbpmn pages; that shortfall is a class-coverage hole upstream, not a failure of this row's fusion. **The two arrowhead detectors are not interchangeable and combining them is negative**: 3.2.6's geometric heads reach **0.6453 alone on hdbpmn** - real signal, well behind the learned class - and adding them to `head_det` **costs 0.0010**, because once the learned class is present the geometric false positives outvote the true ones. **The flow prior, measured rather than assumed, confirms only half of the folklore**: hdbpmn runs left-to-right **76.8%** of the time but has **no vertical convention at all - 50.8%, a coin** - which is what BPMN's notation actually says, so a "top-to-bottom" prior would have been a fabricated feature. The prior alone is the control at **0.7956**, and the full combination beats it by **+0.1152** on hdbpmn but only **+0.0286** on fa_bresler. Roles add nothing over the prior on hdbpmn (identical to four decimals) and do real work on fa_bresler (+0.1911 over heads alone). **The most useful result is an accident in the control**: `id_order`, included as a chance floor, measures **0.7006 on fa_bresler rather than 0.5000** - the corpus's node ids themselves correlate with edge direction 70% of the time - so **every fa_bresler figure must be read against a 0.70 floor and not a 0.50 one**, against which its 0.7611 is worth only about 0.06 of genuine lift. The docstring had been written before any run and every number in it was invented; all of them were replaced from the real run, including its claim that no such id-order effect existed. **Now fitted, run and wired - it had never been either.** `experiments/assemble/direction.json` did not exist, so every predicted edge reached `irdiff` as `directed=False` while the truth records directed edges; since `_edge_keys` keys the two differently, **1,194 of 2,499 val edges (52.6% of hdbpmn's, 23.5% of fa_bresler's) could not be matched at any tracing quality**. `s5._orient` closes that. The measured value is smaller than the defect sounds: **median 9.0 → 7.0 but only +1 page at ≤ 3**, and an oracle that orients every edge perfectly reaches the same 7.0 - so the residual is not fusion error but pages failing by margins no orientation can close | ✅ |
| 10.1.6 | Edge-label binding | Labels near edge midpoint → `yes` / `no` / condition / cardinality | `src/assemble/edgelabels.py`: binding a floating text region to the *edge* it annotates, then typing it, both scored against ground-truth polylines so this stage is isolated from 10.1.3's tracing error - **the real end-to-end number is this F1 times tracing accuracy, not 0.3145 on its own**, and the docstring says so rather than letting the isolated figure stand as the result. **Both of the plan's assumptions in this row are wrong and the corpus says so.** The distance-to-polyline rule beats the plan's literal *nearest edge by midpoint* baseline by **0.2037 F1 (0.3145 against 0.1108)**, nearly tripling it, because the midpoint premise does not hold: measuring where labels actually sit as a normalised arc position gives **mean 0.396, median 0.360, with only 16.67% of correctly bound labels within 0.1 of the true midpoint and 50.67% out in the outer thirds** - they cluster *before* the midpoint, which is what a label drawn beside a gateway branch rather than centred on a long L-shaped flow looks like. And the plan's four categories are not the corpus's: over 1,637 labelled edges and 450 distinct strings, **`cardinality` is entirely unpopulated at 0 of 1,637** - no source here is an ER diagram, so this is corpus coverage rather than a bug - while fa_bresler's single-character automaton alphabet forced a fifth `symbol` class covering **48.14%** of all labels against `condition`'s 50.34% and a combined `yes`/`no` share of just **1.28%**. **Typing is nearly solved once the binding is right, at 0.9867**, so the difficulty in this row is entirely in the binding. The per-source split is stark and diagnosed rather than averaged: **hdbpmn 0.6296, fa_bresler 0.2956, flowchartseg 0.0000** - the last having no labelled edges at all - and fa_bresler is hard *because* its labels are single characters, which cannot use the near-match OCR recovery the `MIN_NEAR_LEN` gate requires, while **86% of its ground truth repeats the same string across several edges**, which makes the correctness criterion generous there by construction. **Two negative results are reported as unproven rather than as passes**: node-theft is zero, but by construction, since the guard refuses regions already owned by a node before scoring ever happens; and the contested parallel-flow case never arose in the corpus sample, so it is verified by a constructed test instead of by evidence | ✅ |
| 10.1.7 | Crossing disambiguation | Distinguish crossing lines from true junctions via curvature continuity | `src/assemble/crossings.py`: at a skeleton junction two strokes either *cross* - two unrelated edges that merely overlap on the page - or *join*, and getting it wrong silently welds two unrelated edges into one, which is the worst failure mode available in Phase 10 because it yields a confident wrong graph rather than a missing one. The discriminator pairs branches by directional continuity and antiparallelism, with the pairing solved **optimally rather than greedily** (a hand-built case where taking branch 0's single best partner forces a worse total is in the tests). Labelled sites come from the ground-truth polylines themselves: two distinct edges whose polylines intersect are a crossing, two sharing an endpoint node and meeting near it are a junction. **Crossings are real but heavily concentrated: 315 crossings against 1,730 junctions over 2,045 sites, on 97 of 470 pages - and 314 of the 315 are hdbpmn**, with fa_bresler contributing exactly one and didi, flowchartseg and sketch2code contributing none at all, since their ground truth carries no polyline geometry to intersect. At the chosen 35° tolerance: **accuracy 0.8704, crossing recall 0.6984, junction recall 0.9017**, and the dominant error is the *safe* direction only by luck - **170 junctions read as crossings against 95 crossings read as junctions**. The tolerance is chosen against a sweep and the choice is argued rather than assumed: raw accuracy actually **peaks at 10° (0.9051)**, but crossing recall keeps climbing to 40°, so 35° is kept as the recall trade and the higher-scoring setting is reported beside it. **The finding that matters is where it breaks, and it is not where intuition puts it**: bucketing real crossings by their acute stroke angle gives **0.8839 recall for near-perpendicular crossings (60-90°, 224 of 315 cases) but 0.1333 at 30-45° and 0.2444 at 45-60°** - the discriminator collapses in the *oblique middle*, not at the extremes, because it was effectively built for the perpendicular case that dominates the labelled set. The oblique diagonal crossing common in hdbpmn's lane-crossing flows (75 of 315 cases) should not be trusted without further work, and the docstring says so. Verified against a synthetic suite of exactly-known geometries - clean X, clean T, shallow and curved crossings, Y-branch - plus odd-branch-count handling; **the original four-way-merge synthetic was found to be geometrically indistinguishable from a shallow crossing and was replaced rather than deleted** | ✅ |
| 10.1.8 | Containment / nesting | Build parent-child tree for wireframe layout | `src/assemble/nesting.py` + `src/assemble/corpus.py` (Phase 10's shared foundation: the 470 held-out pages, one coordinate frame, and 24,385 cached detections). **The plan points this row at a corpus that owns the structure and not the pixels, and that is verified before anything else was written**: sketch2code carries containment as `attrs.kind == "contains"` - **43,649 relations over 44,133 nodes** - and **every one of those 44,133 nodes has `bbox: null`, with all 484 files marked `meta.geometry == "absent"`**, because the IR is converted from the HTML behind each wireframe and nothing was ever rendered. So a single accuracy quoted over "nesting" would be a number about nothing, and the two halves are split and each measured where it is honest. **Half one, the tree builder, on sketch2code's 484 pages**: all 484 yield a well-formed single-rooted tree, **0 need repair** - no cycle, no multi-parent, no second root anywhere in 43,649 relations, so the repair rules are dead code on this corpus and are reported as such rather than as a pass. The number to carry forward is the shape: **max depth 20, mean 5.85, p99 16, median branching 1** - a wireframe nests **twenty levels deep** where a flowchart does not, so anything downstream that recurses over this structure recurses 20 deep. **Half two, the geometric parent finder, on the 242 held-out hdbpmn pages**, whose pools, lanes and subprocesses are real nested rectangles with real boxes: **ceiling 0.9941 on ground-truth boxes, 0.9226 on detected ones - the detector costs 0.0715**, and nodes whose own box was never detected are excluded from that figure and counted separately rather than being scored as parent errors. Two controls keep it honest: **28.92% of nodes genuinely have no parent, so proposing nothing at all scores 0.2892**, and **choosing the outermost enclosing box instead of the innermost collapses to 0.6382, a loss of 0.3559** - which is what proves the innermost rule is doing the work rather than the class prior. The `COVER` threshold is chosen by a sweep (0.7-0.99) that moves detected accuracy by 0.0063, so the constant is not load-bearing. **The honest limit is stated rather than papered over**: the depth-20 distribution and the 0.9226 come from different corpora, because the only corpus with wireframe nesting has no geometry and the only corpus with nested geometry is BPMN, so nothing here says 0.9226 transfers to a wireframe | ✅ |

### 10.2 Validation & Normalization

| # | Task | Detail | Definition of Done | Status |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---: |
| 10.2.1 | Type-specific validators | Flowchart: one start, ≥1 end, decisions ≥2 out. State: reachability. ER: entities have ≥1 attribute. Wireframe: no orphan widgets | `src/assemble/validate.py`: eight rules, each returning `{rule, severity, refs, detail}` with a stable id so 10.2.2 can repair and Phase 16 can point at the offending node. Then the part that matters - **all eight are run over the 5,796 ground-truth IR files, because the plan's rules are asserted rather than tested**. Every rule declares the evidence it needs (`roles`, `edges`) plus a guard, and a rule that lacks it is **skipped, never scored as a pass**: **4,319 files - 74.5% of the corpus - can answer no rule at all** (didi and flowchartseg carry `unknown` on every node and flowchartseg has zero edges by construction), and separating *unanswerable* from *passing* is what stops a corpus artefact reading as a clean bill of health. **Two of the plan's four assertions do not survive.** "Decisions ≥2 out" fires on **58.3% of hand-drawn BPMN** (404 of 693 pages, 541 nodes), which is not a defect rate but a wrong rule: **516 of those 541 - 95.4% - have in-degree ≥2**, so they are *merging* gateways, which have one output by design and which BPMN draws as the same diamond. Adding the only local discriminator, in-degree ≤1, gives `FC.DECISION_SPLIT` at **3.46% - a factor of 17**. Both stay registered: the plan's version as a warning whose *rate is the finding*, the qualified one as the error a repair may act on. **This is also the retrospective diagnosis of 7.3.10**, whose `missing_branch` repair was mostly firing on merges. **"One start" fails in the opposite direction**: of its 160 hits, **158 pages have several starts and only 2 have none**, so multiple triggers are ordinary BPMN and "at least one start" would fire at **0.29%**. Of the rest, **`SM.INITIAL_ONE`'s 17.7% is a label hole rather than a defect** - all 53 are *zero* initial states, never two - so reachability is guarded to the 247 automata that have one; **`SM.REACHABLE` is the only rule that found a genuine defect, 1 in 247**; **`WF.ORPHAN_WIDGET` at 0 of 484 is unfalsified rather than confirmed**, since sketch2code's containment comes from a DOM walk and holds by construction; and **`ER.ENTITY_ATTRS` has a denominator of zero** - the corpus contains no ER diagram - so it reports `null` and not `0.0`, because a rule that was never evaluated and a rule that never fired are not the same claim | ✅ |
